Software Product Management English

A software product-management English curriculum for discovery, roadmaps, user stories, prioritization, metrics, launches, stakeholder negotiation, experimentation, and product strategy.

Words into action

For software product managers, product owners, UX leads, engineering managers, growth product teams, platform PMs, and product-operations staff.

What you will practice · Choose your pace

What you will practice

  • Reframe a requested interface addition using supplied support evidence and agree a focused discovery task.
  • Apply agreed priorities to a capacity conflict, explain the displaced work, and distinguish a review date from a delivery promise.
  • Turn a vague performance request into a checkable criterion and distinguish that criterion from the wider completion standard.
  • Explain opposing funnel movements using aligned cohorts, counts, rates, and an explicit limit on causal interpretation.
  • Discuss interim experiment results without changing the decision rule after seeing favorable data, while preserving guardrail monitoring.

Choose your pace

Quick practice · 15 minutes: complete one short exchange, check the explanations, and read both roles aloud.

Extended practice · two or three sittings: check the case facts, study the vocabulary and phrases, complete the longer conversation, then rehearse the follow-up exchange.

Level guide: designed for intermediate to advanced learners. B1 learners can start with the short exchanges and supplied phrases; B2 learners can complete the cloze conversations; C1 learners can focus on the distinctions in the language checks. These are teaching suggestions, not a certified level assessment.

Prompts

Original fictional English practice, not a product-management certification, statistical analysis service, software security assessment, or release authorization. All teams, data, dates, and decisions are invented. Actual work must follow the organization's responsibilities, research safeguards, engineering controls, experiment methods, and applicable requirements.

01Product Discovery and Problem FramingOne button request, two different problems+

Your goal

Reframe a requested interface addition using supplied support evidence and agree a focused discovery task.

This lesson in the book, page 4 ↗

A Read the situation

One button request, two different problems

Support lead Morgan asks product manager Li for a new Export button on the home page. Their review finds 12 relevant tickets from the past month: eight users could not find the existing action, while four found it but lacked the required permission. The records do not show how common either problem is among all users. No design change is approved. Morgan and Li agree to separate the two issues and prepare a two-day investigation of navigation and permission messaging before choosing a solution.

Morgan: Support lead / Li: Product manager

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What do eight tickets describe?

Answer and explanation

B. Difficulty finding the existing Export action

Eight records concern discoverability, while four describe permission restrictions.

2. What do the other four describe?

Answer and explanation

A. The action was found, but the required permission was missing.

Those four users reached the action but could not use it under their permissions.

3. What have Morgan and Li agreed to do?

Answer and explanation

B. Prepare a two-day investigation of navigation and permission messaging.

The agreement is a bounded investigation before selecting or approving a design change.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Twelve tickets are support evidence, not a census of all users. Eight describe finding the action, and four describe permission. A new button might help the first group but would not itself give the second group authorization to export.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
product discovery
Work to understand problems and test potential product directions.conduct product discovery
problem statement
A concise description of a user problem and its context.frame the problem statement
solution request
A request for a particular implementation or feature.unpack the solution request
user need
An outcome a user needs to accomplish.identify the user need
job to be done
The progress or task a user seeks in a given situation.clarify the job to be done
user segment
A group of users sharing relevant characteristics or needs.define the user segment
support evidence
Information from support interactions used to investigate problems.review support evidence
discoverability
How readily users can find an available function.improve discoverability
permission
Authorization to perform a defined action.explain the permission requirement
access barrier
A condition preventing a user from reaching or using a capability.identify the access barrier
navigation path
The sequence of locations used to reach a function.trace the navigation path
friction point
A step that makes a user task harder or less reliable.locate a friction point
usability test
Observation of representative users attempting defined tasks.run a usability test
task success
Completion of a defined user task under stated conditions.measure task success
research question
A specific uncertainty the investigation aims to resolve.define the research question
assumption
An unverified premise guiding a proposal or decision.test the assumption
hypothesis
A proposed explanation or prediction to evaluate.state the hypothesis
prototype
A representation used to explore or test a design.compare prototypes
desirability
Whether a solution addresses something users value.assess desirability
feasibility
Whether a solution can be built and operated under constraints.assess feasibility
viability
Whether a solution can support the relevant business needs.assess viability
opportunity
A problem or possibility that could create product value.evaluate the opportunity
discovery scope
The defined boundaries of the investigation.bound the discovery scope
evidence gap
Missing information needed to support a conclusion.identify the evidence gap

The two-day scope creates a decision point without predetermining a design. The investigation can examine navigation and permission messaging separately. Its purpose is not to remove legitimate access controls or claim that the requested button is already proven necessary.

C Notice the language

Clarification is not rejection

A stakeholder may offer a solution because it makes a recurring problem tangible. Acknowledge the problem, then separate the proposed implementation from the evidence. Agree a small next step so the conversation does not feel like an indefinite research delay.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the problem
We should address the export friction in these tickets.
Separate request from need
The button is one proposed solution, not the problem itself.
Identify the task
What were users trying to export, and from where?
Split the evidence
Eight tickets concern finding the action; four concern permission.
Name the first issue
The existing action may have a discoverability problem.
Name the second issue
The permission message may need a separate review.
Avoid a false fix
Adding a button would not grant authorization.
Limit the inference
These tickets do not establish prevalence across all users.
Ask a focused question
Can users find the action during the relevant task?
Keep safeguards
We are not proposing to bypass access controls.
Bound the investigation
Let us spend two days examining these two paths.
Request useful detail
Link each ticket to the task and navigation path.
Compare possibilities
A clearer label and a new location are different options.
Preserve decision status
No design change is approved yet.
Agree the handoff
Bring the observed paths and findings to the next review.
Close with purpose
Choose the solution after separating the two problems.
6 language and precision notes
Request
May contain a solution before the underlying need is clear.
Users
Specify which users and what evidence supports the claim.
Cannot
Distinguish cannot find from is not permitted to use.
Discoverable
A function existing does not mean people can locate it.
Research
Give it a question, scope, and decision point.
Validate
State what evidence would support or challenge the assumption.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which problem framing fits the tickets?

Answer and explanation

D. Users encounter two barriers: finding Export and understanding or obtaining the required permission.

The evidence separates discoverability and permission without assuming one implementation solves both.

2. Which statement overclaims the ticket evidence?

Answer and explanation

C. Every user is unable to export.

The reviewed tickets do not establish the experience or prevalence of the entire user population.

3. Which discovery task is appropriately focused?

Answer and explanation

D. Observe the export path and review permission messaging for the two ticket groups.

The task addresses the two identified barriers and can inform a bounded solution decision.

4. Why is adding a button not a complete answer for the four permission cases?

Answer and explanation

A. Finding an action and being authorized to use it are separate conditions.

Those users already located Export, so another location would not itself grant the missing permission.

E Conversations

One button request, two different problems

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Morgan: Support lead / Li: Product manager

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • research question
  • prototype
  • user need
  • support evidence
  • discovery scope
  • navigation path
  • problem statement
  • solution request
  • discoverability
  • permission
  1. Morgan
    Can we put an Export button on the home page? Support keeps hearing that people cannot export, and I would like something visible we can point them to.
  2. Li
    I want to address that, but let us separate the 1 from the problem. The existing action already exports, so what happened when these users tried to reach it?
  3. Morgan
    We reviewed twelve tickets from last month. Eight users could not find the action. Four found it but did not have the required permission.
  4. Li
    Then 2 is one issue, not the entire set. We should keep the four permission cases separate instead of treating cannot export as a single diagnosis.
  5. Morgan
    So the button might help eight of them, but it would leave the other four stuck. Can we keep those groups separate in the ticket summary?
  6. Li
    Right. Their 3 problem needs its own review. We can examine the explanation and request path without suggesting that legitimate access controls should disappear.
  7. Morgan
    I can attach the relevant ticket excerpts and task details. We should not just count the phrase cannot export when it can describe different experiences.
  8. Li
    That makes the 4 more useful. It still does not tell us how common either problem is across all users, so keep that broader claim out of the summary.
  9. Morgan
    For the navigation cases, I would like to know where people started and which menu labels they tried. That could explain why the existing location was missed.
  10. Li
    Trace the 5 during the actual task. A clearer label, a different location, and an additional button are distinct options rather than interchangeable fixes.
  11. Morgan
    How much investigation are we talking about? Support needs a date for an update, even if we cannot promise a fix yet.
  12. Li
    Let us set a two-day 6. We will examine these two paths and bring the findings back before choosing a design change.
  13. Morgan
    For the navigation work, the question should be whether people can find the action when they need it, not whether they like our preferred button idea.
  14. Li
    That is a better 7. We can observe what happens and compare the evidence with the assumption behind the request.
  15. Morgan
    The permission review should also check whether the message explains why access is unavailable and what the legitimate next step is. It should not promise access automatically.
  16. Li
    Agreed. Keep the 8 separate from a specific control. The product should help users understand the path without disguising an authorization decision as a navigation change.
  17. Morgan
    Once we have the observations, we can decide whether to test a revised label, a new location, or another design. We do not have to build every option.
  18. Li
    A 9 may help compare the relevant direction before implementation. The choice should follow what we learn, not merely the most visible suggestion in the initial request.
  19. Morgan
    I will prepare the ticket groups and coordinate the review. Our status will say that we have agreed the investigation, not approved a new home-page feature.
  20. Li
    Good. Record the 10 with both barriers and the remaining uncertainty. That gives Support a clear next step and gives the product team a useful basis for choosing a solution.

Answers and explanations
  1. solution request Turn 2. The solution request names a new button, while the underlying evidence may support more than one kind of problem.
  2. discoverability Turn 4. Discoverability concerns finding the available action, which is the barrier described in eight of the twelve tickets.
  3. permission Turn 6. Permission is authorization to use the action, distinct from being able to locate it.
  4. support evidence Turn 8. Support evidence provides concrete reported experiences, but the ticket sample does not establish prevalence among all users.
  5. navigation path Turn 10. The navigation path records the sequence users followed and can reveal where they missed the existing action.
  6. discovery scope Turn 12. Discovery scope bounds the work to navigation and permission messaging, creating a specific decision point before design selection.
  7. research question Turn 14. The research question tests the user's task and obstacle instead of soliciting approval of a predetermined button solution.
  8. user need Turn 16. The user need concerns accomplishing the export task appropriately, rather than mandating a specific interface element.
  9. prototype Turn 18. A prototype can test a proposed design before committing to full implementation, using the findings from the bounded investigation.
  10. problem statement Turn 20. The problem statement separates the two observed barriers and gives the next design discussion an evidence-based starting point.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Morgan: Can we put an Export button on the home page? Support keeps hearing that people cannot export, and I would like something visible we can point them to.
  2. Li: I want to address that, but let us separate the solution request from the problem. The existing action already exports, so what happened when these users tried to reach it?
  3. Morgan: We reviewed twelve tickets from last month. Eight users could not find the action. Four found it but did not have the required permission.
  4. Li: Then discoverability is one issue, not the entire set. We should keep the four permission cases separate instead of treating cannot export as a single diagnosis.
  5. Morgan: So the button might help eight of them, but it would leave the other four stuck. Can we keep those groups separate in the ticket summary?
  6. Li: Right. Their permission problem needs its own review. We can examine the explanation and request path without suggesting that legitimate access controls should disappear.
  7. Morgan: I can attach the relevant ticket excerpts and task details. We should not just count the phrase cannot export when it can describe different experiences.
  8. Li: That makes the support evidence more useful. It still does not tell us how common either problem is across all users, so keep that broader claim out of the summary.
  9. Morgan: For the navigation cases, I would like to know where people started and which menu labels they tried. That could explain why the existing location was missed.
  10. Li: Trace the navigation path during the actual task. A clearer label, a different location, and an additional button are distinct options rather than interchangeable fixes.
  11. Morgan: How much investigation are we talking about? Support needs a date for an update, even if we cannot promise a fix yet.
  12. Li: Let us set a two-day discovery scope. We will examine these two paths and bring the findings back before choosing a design change.
  13. Morgan: For the navigation work, the question should be whether people can find the action when they need it, not whether they like our preferred button idea.
  14. Li: That is a better research question. We can observe what happens and compare the evidence with the assumption behind the request.
  15. Morgan: The permission review should also check whether the message explains why access is unavailable and what the legitimate next step is. It should not promise access automatically.
  16. Li: Agreed. Keep the user need separate from a specific control. The product should help users understand the path without disguising an authorization decision as a navigation change.
  17. Morgan: Once we have the observations, we can decide whether to test a revised label, a new location, or another design. We do not have to build every option.
  18. Li: A prototype may help compare the relevant direction before implementation. The choice should follow what we learn, not merely the most visible suggestion in the initial request.
  19. Morgan: I will prepare the ticket groups and coordinate the review. Our status will say that we have agreed the investigation, not approved a new home-page feature.
  20. Li: Good. Record the problem statement with both barriers and the remaining uncertainty. That gives Support a clear next step and gives the product team a useful basis for choosing a solution.
Dialogue in the book, page 10 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Find the action before adding another button

Fictional discovery discussion using the lesson case: a stakeholder requests a button, while support notes describe difficulty finding an existing action.

  1. Sales stakeholder: Could you add a new export button to the home screen in the next release?
  2. Product manager: Which user need would it address? Support notes mention difficulty finding the existing export action.
  3. Sales stakeholder: Customers ask our team where to download their records. I assumed another button would solve that.
  4. Product manager: That suggests a discoverability problem, but we haven't established the best solution or affected persona yet.
  5. Sales stakeholder: Could we look at the support examples before deciding where any new control belongs?
  6. Product manager: Yes. Let's clarify who was trying to export, from which page, and for what use case.
  7. Sales stakeholder: I don't have those details here. I'll bring the original notes rather than invent a typical customer.
  8. Product manager: Good. We should also consider opportunity cost before committing development capacity to a proposed solution.
  9. Sales stakeholder: I'll describe the request as an export-discovery question, not an approved home-screen feature.
  10. Product manager: Then we can investigate the problem and compare possible responses with the evidence in view.
Who needs the weekly digest?

Fictional discovery interview: a department lead requests a weekly email digest but has not identified recipients or decisions it should support.

  1. Department lead: Our product needs a weekly email digest. Can that go straight into the backlog?
  2. Product researcher: What decision would recipients make from it, and which people would actually receive the message?
  3. Department lead: I had managers in mind, but I haven't asked whether they need weekly summaries.
  4. Product researcher: Then the recipient persona and frequency are assumptions. Could we explore the underlying reporting task first?
  5. Department lead: Managers sometimes ask for project updates. I don't know whether email is their preferred channel.
  6. Product researcher: That's useful context, not confirmation of a digest use case. Can we speak with those managers?
  7. Department lead: Yes. I'll introduce you and avoid promising a feature before we understand their workflow.
  8. Product researcher: I'll ask what they currently review, what is missing, and what happens when information arrives late.
  9. Department lead: Can the discovery conversation also reveal whether an existing view already meets part of the need?
  10. Product researcher: Absolutely. That helps us compare options and avoid spending capacity before the problem is clear.
A competitor feature is not a user problem

Fictional roadmap intake: a stakeholder asks for color-coded folders because a competitor offers them; no customer use case has been established.

  1. Marketing lead: A competitor has color-coded folders. Should we match that feature before our next announcement?
  2. Product manager: What user problem would colors solve in our product, beyond making the feature lists look similar?
  3. Marketing lead: Possibly faster organization, but that's my interpretation rather than something customers have told us.
  4. Product manager: Then let's keep it as a hypothesis and identify a concrete organization task to investigate.
  5. Marketing lead: Could we ask people who manage many folders how they currently find the right one?
  6. Product manager: Yes. We should learn their use case and constraints without leading them toward colors as the answer.
  7. Marketing lead: I can help recruit relevant users, although we haven't defined that persona precisely yet.
  8. Product manager: Let's define the recruitment criteria together and consider the opportunity cost of any proposed build.
  9. Marketing lead: I'll hold the launch claim until we have a problem statement and a prioritization decision.
  10. Product manager: Good. Competitive information can prompt a question without dictating the solution we eventually choose.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

One button request, two different problems
  1. Read turns 1-10 in pairs. Stress finding and permission as different barriers.
  2. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Pause after the two-day limit and the no-approval statement.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

A visible action can still be restricted

Ten tickets are reviewed. Six users could not find Download. Four found it but lacked permission. No design change is approved, and the records do not represent all users.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Support: "Six tickets concern ___."

Answer and explanation

C. discoverability

Those users could not find the existing action, which is a discoverability problem.

2. Product: "Four concern ___."

Answer and explanation

D. permission

The other users found Download but lacked authorization to use it.

3. Support: "A design change is not yet ___."

Answer and explanation

A. approved

The briefing explicitly leaves design approval absent at this stage.

4. Product: "These records are a ticket sample, not all ___."

Answer and explanation

C. users

The supplied records do not establish the experience of every user.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Another round with an AI partner

Extend the vocabularyPractice the grammarRehearse with AI

Copy a finished AI prompt for this lesson

These prompts are fully written and include this lesson's facts and language. Open one, copy it as written, and paste it into a new AI chat.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Lesson 01: Product Discovery and Problem Framing
Original fictional case: Support lead Morgan asks product manager Li for a new Export button on the home page. Their review finds 12 relevant tickets from the past month: eight users could not find the existing action, while four found it but lacked the required permission. The records do not show how common either problem is among all users. No design change is approved. Morgan and Li agree to separate the two issues and prepare a two-day investigation of navigation and permission messaging before choosing a solution.
Language workshop: One button request, two different problems
Communication goal: Reframe a requested interface addition using supplied support evidence and agree a focused discovery task.
Language guidance: Twelve tickets are support evidence, not a census of all users. Eight describe finding the action, and four describe permission. A new button might help the first group but would not itself give the second group authorization to export.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): We should address the export friction in these tickets. / The button is one proposed solution, not the problem itself. / What were users trying to export, and from where? / Eight tickets concern finding the action; four concern permission. / The existing action may have a discoverability problem. / The permission message may need a separate review. / Adding a button would not grant authorization. / These tickets do not establish prevalence across all users. / Can users find the action during the relevant task? / We are not proposing to bypass access controls. / Let us spend two days examining these two paths. / Link each ticket to the task and navigation path. / A clearer label and a new location are different options. / No design change is approved yet. / Bring the observed paths and findings to the next review. / Choose the solution after separating the two problems.
Vocabulary: product discovery: Work to understand problems and test potential product directions. / problem statement: A concise description of a user problem and its context. / solution request: A request for a particular implementation or feature. / user need: An outcome a user needs to accomplish. / job to be done: The progress or task a user seeks in a given situation. / user segment: A group of users sharing relevant characteristics or needs. / support evidence: Information from support interactions used to investigate problems. / discoverability: How readily users can find an available function. / permission: Authorization to perform a defined action. / access barrier: A condition preventing a user from reaching or using a capability. / navigation path: The sequence of locations used to reach a function. / friction point: A step that makes a user task harder or less reliable. / usability test: Observation of representative users attempting defined tasks. / task success: Completion of a defined user task under stated conditions. / research question: A specific uncertainty the investigation aims to resolve. / assumption: An unverified premise guiding a proposal or decision. / hypothesis: A proposed explanation or prediction to evaluate. / prototype: A representation used to explore or test a design. / desirability: Whether a solution addresses something users value. / feasibility: Whether a solution can be built and operated under constraints. / viability: Whether a solution can support the relevant business needs. / opportunity: A problem or possibility that could create product value. / discovery scope: The defined boundaries of the investigation. / evidence gap: Missing information needed to support a conclusion.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10 in pairs. Stress finding and permission as different barriers. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Pause after the two-day limit and the no-approval statement. Check the Download exchange, then read it with six and four clearly distinguished.
Partner: Morgan: Support lead / Li: Product manager
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Ten tickets are reviewed. Six users could not find Download. Four found it but lacked permission. No design change is approved, and the records do not represent all users.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Morgan: Once we have the observations, we can decide whether to test a revised label, a new location, or another design. We do not have to build every option.
Li: A prototype may help compare the relevant direction before implementation. The choice should follow what we learn, not merely the most visible suggestion in the initial request.
Morgan: I will prepare the ticket groups and coordinate the review. Our status will say that we have agreed the investigation, not approved a new home-page feature.
Li: Good. Record the problem statement with both barriers and the remaining uncertainty. That gives Support a clear next step and gives the product team a useful basis for choosing a solution.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Lesson 01: Product Discovery and Problem Framing
Original fictional case: Support lead Morgan asks product manager Li for a new Export button on the home page. Their review finds 12 relevant tickets from the past month: eight users could not find the existing action, while four found it but lacked the required permission. The records do not show how common either problem is among all users. No design change is approved. Morgan and Li agree to separate the two issues and prepare a two-day investigation of navigation and permission messaging before choosing a solution.
Language workshop: One button request, two different problems
Communication goal: Reframe a requested interface addition using supplied support evidence and agree a focused discovery task.
Language guidance: Twelve tickets are support evidence, not a census of all users. Eight describe finding the action, and four describe permission. A new button might help the first group but would not itself give the second group authorization to export.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): We should address the export friction in these tickets. / The button is one proposed solution, not the problem itself. / What were users trying to export, and from where? / Eight tickets concern finding the action; four concern permission. / The existing action may have a discoverability problem. / The permission message may need a separate review. / Adding a button would not grant authorization. / These tickets do not establish prevalence across all users. / Can users find the action during the relevant task? / We are not proposing to bypass access controls. / Let us spend two days examining these two paths. / Link each ticket to the task and navigation path. / A clearer label and a new location are different options. / No design change is approved yet. / Bring the observed paths and findings to the next review. / Choose the solution after separating the two problems.
Vocabulary: product discovery: Work to understand problems and test potential product directions. / problem statement: A concise description of a user problem and its context. / solution request: A request for a particular implementation or feature. / user need: An outcome a user needs to accomplish. / job to be done: The progress or task a user seeks in a given situation. / user segment: A group of users sharing relevant characteristics or needs. / support evidence: Information from support interactions used to investigate problems. / discoverability: How readily users can find an available function. / permission: Authorization to perform a defined action. / access barrier: A condition preventing a user from reaching or using a capability. / navigation path: The sequence of locations used to reach a function. / friction point: A step that makes a user task harder or less reliable. / usability test: Observation of representative users attempting defined tasks. / task success: Completion of a defined user task under stated conditions. / research question: A specific uncertainty the investigation aims to resolve. / assumption: An unverified premise guiding a proposal or decision. / hypothesis: A proposed explanation or prediction to evaluate. / prototype: A representation used to explore or test a design. / desirability: Whether a solution addresses something users value. / feasibility: Whether a solution can be built and operated under constraints. / viability: Whether a solution can support the relevant business needs. / opportunity: A problem or possibility that could create product value. / discovery scope: The defined boundaries of the investigation. / evidence gap: Missing information needed to support a conclusion.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10 in pairs. Stress finding and permission as different barriers. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Pause after the two-day limit and the no-approval statement. Check the Download exchange, then read it with six and four clearly distinguished.
Partner: Morgan: Support lead / Li: Product manager
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Ten tickets are reviewed. Six users could not find Download. Four found it but lacked permission. No design change is approved, and the records do not represent all users.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Morgan: Once we have the observations, we can decide whether to test a revised label, a new location, or another design. We do not have to build every option.
Li: A prototype may help compare the relevant direction before implementation. The choice should follow what we learn, not merely the most visible suggestion in the initial request.
Morgan: I will prepare the ticket groups and coordinate the review. Our status will say that we have agreed the investigation, not approved a new home-page feature.
Li: Good. Record the problem statement with both barriers and the remaining uncertainty. That gives Support a clear next step and gives the product team a useful basis for choosing a solution.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

02Roadmaps and PrioritizationThree urgent requests, two delivery slots+

Your goal

Apply agreed priorities to a capacity conflict, explain the displaced work, and distinguish a review date from a delivery promise.

This lesson in the book, page 13 ↗

A Read the situation

Three urgent requests, two delivery slots

Product owner Nora and commercial sponsor Samir review three features. A improves setup recovery and directly supports the agreed activation goal. B provides audit export needed for a recorded October 31 customer commitment. C refreshes the home page; no fixed commitment or activation evidence is supplied for it. Each feature needs one of two available delivery slots, and splitting them is not feasible this cycle. The agreed rule is to protect fixed commitments first, then the activation goal. Nora proposes B and A, with C reviewed again in two weeks.

Nora: Product owner / Samir: Commercial sponsor

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which feature addresses the recorded fixed commitment?

Answer and explanation

C. B, audit export

The briefing identifies B as required for the October 31 customer commitment.

2. Which pair follows the agreed priority rule?

Answer and explanation

B. B and A

B protects the fixed commitment, and A then supports the agreed activation goal.

3. What does the two-week date mean for C?

Answer and explanation

D. A review point, not a promised delivery date

Nora proposes reviewing C again in two weeks, not delivering or approving it then.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

With two slots and three one-slot features, selecting C displaces A or B. Under the supplied rule, B comes first because of the recorded commitment, followed by A because it supports activation. No evidence permits treating all three as simultaneously feasible.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
product roadmap
A communication of intended product direction and priorities over time.update the product roadmap
product goal
The future product outcome guiding a defined body of work.align with the product goal
prioritization criterion
A stated basis for ordering competing work.apply prioritization criteria
capacity constraint
A limit on work the team can undertake in the period.state the capacity constraint
delivery slot
A defined allocation of capacity for planned work.allocate a delivery slot
fixed commitment
A recorded obligation treated as non-discretionary in the stated planning rule.protect a fixed commitment
time-critical work
Work whose value or obligation depends on a specific timing need.identify time-critical work
opportunity cost
The value of the best displaced alternative.explain opportunity cost
trade-off
A choice that gains one benefit while giving up another.make the trade-off explicit
sequencing
Ordering work to respect priorities and dependencies.agree the sequencing
dependency
An input or condition required for another item to proceed.identify a dependency
deferred item
Work deliberately moved outside the current period.record a deferred item
backlog ordering
Arranging planned work by current priority.revise backlog ordering
sponsor
A stakeholder advocating or accountable for a proposed investment.brief the sponsor
escalation
Raising a decision beyond the current authority or agreement.define the escalation route
decision rule
An agreed method for selecting among alternatives.use the decision rule
decision log
A record of choices, rationale, and relevant context.maintain the decision log
scope split
Dividing work into independently useful smaller parts.assess a scope split
confidence level
Stated certainty in an estimate or claim under its defined basis.qualify the confidence level
effort estimate
An assessment of the work needed to deliver an item.review the effort estimate
cost of delay
The consequence of postponing work over a stated period.assess cost of delay
review trigger
A condition prompting reconsideration of a decision.define the review trigger
planning horizon
The period covered by a planning discussion.state the planning horizon
forecast
A current estimate of a future result, not necessarily a commitment.distinguish forecast from commitment

A review in two weeks is not a promise to deliver C in two weeks or in the next cycle. New evidence can reopen the priority decision, but it must be evaluated against the same capacity and commitments unless those constraints are explicitly changed.

C Notice the language

Make the deferred work visible

A clear no for this cycle can be more useful than an impossible yes. Explain the shared criterion, the capacity constraint, and the work that would be displaced by a change. Give the deferred request an honest review point without quietly promising the next release.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
State the capacity
We have two slots for three one-slot features.
Name the shared rule
Fixed commitments come first, then the activation goal.
Protect the commitment
B addresses the recorded October 31 commitment.
Connect the outcome
A directly supports setup recovery and activation.
Qualify the third request
C has no supplied fixed commitment or activation evidence.
Make the selection
On this basis, I recommend B and A.
Name the consequence
C moves outside this cycle.
Ask what gives way
Which selected item would C displace?
Check splitting honestly
The team has assessed splitting as infeasible this cycle.
Avoid false capacity
Calling all three urgent does not create another slot.
Invite relevant evidence
What new fact would change C's priority under our rule?
Separate appeal from decision
I can record your objection without pretending the priority changed.
Define reconsideration
We will review C again in two weeks.
Avoid a hidden promise
That review date is not a release commitment.
Record the basis
Put the selected pair and displaced work in the decision log.
Close consistently
Communicate the same scope and timing status to every sponsor.
6 language and precision notes
Urgent
Ask for the actual timing consequence or obligation.
Priority
Meaningful only when it changes the order or allocation.
Next
Avoid implying the deferred item is automatically next to ship.
Capacity
A constraint to verify, not a number changed by enthusiasm.
Review
A decision checkpoint, not necessarily approval or delivery.
Committed
Distinguish a recorded obligation from an internal preference.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which response best explains deferring C?

Answer and explanation

B. B protects the fixed commitment and A supports activation; C does not fit the two-slot capacity.

The explanation applies the supplied rule and states the capacity consequence without personal dismissal or an invented date.

2. If C is added with no capacity change, what must happen?

Answer and explanation

B. A or B must be displaced.

Each feature requires one slot and the briefing rules out splitting, so another selection must change.

3. What new information could justify reconsideration?

Answer and explanation

A. Relevant evidence changing C's position under the agreed criteria

A priority decision can change with relevant evidence, not merely repeated preference.

4. Which statement about the review is accurate?

Answer and explanation

C. C will be reconsidered in two weeks without a promised delivery date.

A reconsideration checkpoint preserves flexibility without creating an unsupported release commitment.

E Conversations

Three urgent requests, two delivery slots

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Nora: Product owner / Samir: Commercial sponsor

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank

  1. Samir
    All three sponsors are calling their feature urgent. My team wants the home-page refresh included because we have already discussed it in several customer conversations.
  2. Nora
    We have a 1. There are two slots, and the team has assessed each feature at one slot. We need to choose rather than announce a scope the team cannot deliver.
  3. Samir
    Understood. But I need more than "there is no room" for the sponsor. Can you walk me through the rule we agreed?
  4. Nora
    The agreed 2 is fixed commitments first, then the activation goal. It gives us a shared basis instead of comparing the intensity of each sponsor's request.
  5. Samir
    The audit export has the October thirty-first customer commitment. That is different from my team's interest in showing a more current home page.
  6. Nora
    Yes. B protects that 3. We should not displace it without explicitly addressing the obligation and the required authority, rather than treating it as another optional preference.
  7. Samir
    That leaves one slot. Setup recovery directly supports activation, which is the outcome we agreed to improve this cycle. We have not supplied similar evidence for the refresh.
  8. Nora
    A therefore aligns with the 4. My recommendation is B and A, with C outside this cycle. That is a relative priority decision, not a claim that C can never matter.
  9. Samir
    Could we include a smaller refresh alongside the other two? I want to test that option before telling the sponsor the request has moved out.
  10. Nora
    We asked about a 5, and the team says it is not feasible this cycle. We cannot count on a smaller item without a supported estimate and usable scope.
  11. Samir
    Then putting C back in would require removing one of the selected features. The trade-off should be stated plainly instead of hidden in an overloaded roadmap.
  12. Nora
    Exactly. Explain the 6. Choosing the refresh means giving up the selected work that would otherwise occupy that slot under our current capacity.
  13. Samir
    I can explain that to the sponsor. How can we bring new evidence back if a specific customer consequence becomes clearer?
  14. Nora
    Set a 7. Relevant new evidence can change the decision, but the review must still account for the fixed commitment, activation goal, and available capacity.
  15. Samir
    Two weeks is a review, then, not a release date? I need to correct that in my customer-call notes before it becomes an expectation.
  16. Nora
    Correct. C is a 8, and the two-week meeting is a review point. It is not automatic approval for the next cycle or a hidden delivery commitment.
  17. Samir
    Please record the rationale for every sponsor. Different informal explanations could make a consistent decision look arbitrary.
  18. Nora
    I will update the 9 with B and A, the capacity limit, and the review point for C. Your concern and the relevant evidence request will be recorded too.
  19. Samir
    Then I will tell the commercial team that the refresh is deferred under the agreed rule, not canceled forever, and that the next discussion needs evidence rather than another urgency label.
  20. Nora
    That keeps the 10 honest. We can pursue the current goal while leaving a clear route to reconsider the deferred request when the facts change.

Answers and explanations
  1. capacity constraint Turn 2. The capacity constraint is two available delivery slots for three one-slot features, with no feasible split this cycle.
  2. decision rule Turn 4. The decision rule protects recorded fixed commitments first and then the activation goal, rather than ranking sponsors by forcefulness.
  3. fixed commitment Turn 6. The fixed commitment is the recorded October 31 audit-export obligation that the supplied rule prioritizes.
  4. product goal Turn 8. The product goal is the agreed activation outcome, which setup recovery directly supports in the supplied facts.
  5. scope split Turn 10. A scope split would divide work into useful smaller parts, but the briefing states it is not feasible this cycle.
  6. opportunity cost Turn 12. Opportunity cost makes the displaced alternative visible when C competes for one of the two available slots.
  7. review trigger Turn 14. A review trigger identifies new evidence or a condition that would justify reconsidering C under the agreed decision rule.
  8. deferred item Turn 16. A deferred item is outside the current cycle; its review date does not guarantee a future delivery slot.
  9. decision log Turn 18. The decision log preserves the selected pair, shared rationale, capacity constraint, and conditions for reconsideration.
  10. product roadmap Turn 20. The product roadmap communicates the selected direction and priorities without implying that every requested feature is simultaneously committed.

Dialogue in the book, page 19 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Three priorities, capacity for two

Separate fictional roadmap case: saved filters, bulk export, and team templates each require four team-weeks. Eight team-weeks are available this quarter. Engineering has also assessed a personal-template slice at two team-weeks. Later capacity and stakeholder approval are unconfirmed.

  1. Portfolio sponsor: Saved filters, bulk export, and team templates are all urgent. Can we deliver all three?
  2. Product manager: They total twelve team-weeks, but this quarter has eight. We need a scope or timing tradeoff.
  3. Portfolio sponsor: Give me two concrete options rather than another request to prioritize everything.
  4. Product manager: Option one: filters and export this quarter, if sponsors agree; propose team templates for a later confirmed slot.
  5. Portfolio sponsor: And an option that gives template users something sooner, even with a narrower scope?
  6. Product manager: Option two: filters plus the assessed personal-template slice, if accepted; defer export and shared templates.
  7. Portfolio sponsor: That uses six weeks. Could the remaining two finish bulk export as well?
  8. Product manager: No, export requires four. Spare capacity doesn't complete that feature; later delivery still needs confirmation.
  9. Portfolio sponsor: I prefer option two, subject to template users accepting personal scope and the export sponsor agreeing.
  10. Product manager: I'll seek those decisions before changing the roadmap; later dates remain uncommitted.
A dependency changes the order, not the goal

Fictional planning: a new reporting view depends on an account-permissions update; both are proposed for the same planning period.

  1. Business sponsor: The reporting view is more visible. Can we build it before the account-permissions update?
  2. Product manager: The proposed view depends on that update. Reversing the order may leave the view unable to work.
  3. Business sponsor: Could the team start the visual design while the permissions dependency is being addressed?
  4. Product manager: That may be possible, but parallel preparation isn't the same as completing the dependent feature first.
  5. Business sponsor: Please show that distinction in the roadmap so stakeholders don't mistake an early mockup for delivery.
  6. Product manager: Agreed. I'll ask the engineering lead which tasks can overlap and which require the dependency.
  7. Business sponsor: If overlap isn't feasible, could we move the reporting commitment rather than pretend capacity increased?
  8. Product manager: Yes. We'll compare that timing option with any reviewed scope reduction that still provides user value.
  9. Business sponsor: I can support a conditional plan, provided the dependency and expected decision point are clear.
  10. Product manager: I'll bring the assessed sequence before we convert the proposal into a delivery commitment.
Urgent intake after the roadmap is agreed

Fictional product planning: a new sponsor requests a bulk-edit feature after the team has committed its available capacity; no estimate exists.

  1. Operations sponsor: We need bulk editing urgently. Can you add it to the already agreed roadmap?
  2. Product manager: We can assess the request, but current capacity is committed and we have no estimate yet.
  3. Operations sponsor: Could we call it a small enhancement so it doesn't displace anything already promised?
  4. Product manager: The label doesn't establish effort. We need to understand the use case, dependencies, and testing scope.
  5. Operations sponsor: Then what can we negotiate now before the estimate is available from engineering?
  6. Product manager: We can clarify urgency and identify which existing outcome you would consider postponing if necessary.
  7. Operations sponsor: I can discuss priorities, but I cannot approve moving another sponsor's committed work alone.
  8. Product manager: I'll bring both sponsors into that tradeoff discussion once we have an assessed option.
  9. Operations sponsor: Please acknowledge the request without telling users that bulk editing has a confirmed delivery date.
  10. Product manager: Agreed. The roadmap changes only after the relevant scope, capacity, and approval questions are resolved.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Three urgent requests, two delivery slots
  1. Read turns 1-10. Emphasize two slots and the fixed-commitment rule.
  2. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Contrast a review point with a delivery promise.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Two slots still mean two choices

There are two slots. X protects a fixed commitment; Y supports the agreed activation goal; Z has neither supplied basis. Each needs one slot, and splitting is infeasible. The rule prioritizes commitments, then activation.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Product: "The first selection is ___."

Answer and explanation

B. X

X protects the fixed commitment, which the stated rule ranks first.

2. Sponsor: "The second selection is ___."

Answer and explanation

D. Y

Y supports activation, the next criterion after fixed commitments.

3. Product: "The deferred item is ___."

Answer and explanation

C. Z

Z has neither supplied priority basis and cannot fit alongside both selected items.

4. Sponsor: "Adding it requires a ___."

Answer and explanation

D. trade-off

With unchanged two-slot capacity, adding Z displaces one of the selected features.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Lesson 02: Roadmaps and Prioritization
Original fictional case: Product owner Nora and commercial sponsor Samir review three features. A improves setup recovery and directly supports the agreed activation goal. B provides audit export needed for a recorded October 31 customer commitment. C refreshes the home page; no fixed commitment or activation evidence is supplied for it. Each feature needs one of two available delivery slots, and splitting them is not feasible this cycle. The agreed rule is to protect fixed commitments first, then the activation goal. Nora proposes B and A, with C reviewed again in two weeks.
Language workshop: Three urgent requests, two delivery slots
Communication goal: Apply agreed priorities to a capacity conflict, explain the displaced work, and distinguish a review date from a delivery promise.
Language guidance: With two slots and three one-slot features, selecting C displaces A or B. Under the supplied rule, B comes first because of the recorded commitment, followed by A because it supports activation. No evidence permits treating all three as simultaneously feasible.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): We have two slots for three one-slot features. / Fixed commitments come first, then the activation goal. / B addresses the recorded October 31 commitment. / A directly supports setup recovery and activation. / C has no supplied fixed commitment or activation evidence. / On this basis, I recommend B and A. / C moves outside this cycle. / Which selected item would C displace? / The team has assessed splitting as infeasible this cycle. / Calling all three urgent does not create another slot. / What new fact would change C's priority under our rule? / I can record your objection without pretending the priority changed. / We will review C again in two weeks. / That review date is not a release commitment. / Put the selected pair and displaced work in the decision log. / Communicate the same scope and timing status to every sponsor.
Vocabulary: product roadmap: A communication of intended product direction and priorities over time. / product goal: The future product outcome guiding a defined body of work. / prioritization criterion: A stated basis for ordering competing work. / capacity constraint: A limit on work the team can undertake in the period. / delivery slot: A defined allocation of capacity for planned work. / fixed commitment: A recorded obligation treated as non-discretionary in the stated planning rule. / time-critical work: Work whose value or obligation depends on a specific timing need. / opportunity cost: The value of the best displaced alternative. / trade-off: A choice that gains one benefit while giving up another. / sequencing: Ordering work to respect priorities and dependencies. / dependency: An input or condition required for another item to proceed. / deferred item: Work deliberately moved outside the current period. / backlog ordering: Arranging planned work by current priority. / sponsor: A stakeholder advocating or accountable for a proposed investment. / escalation: Raising a decision beyond the current authority or agreement. / decision rule: An agreed method for selecting among alternatives. / decision log: A record of choices, rationale, and relevant context. / scope split: Dividing work into independently useful smaller parts. / confidence level: Stated certainty in an estimate or claim under its defined basis. / effort estimate: An assessment of the work needed to deliver an item. / cost of delay: The consequence of postponing work over a stated period. / review trigger: A condition prompting reconsideration of a decision. / planning horizon: The period covered by a planning discussion. / forecast: A current estimate of a future result, not necessarily a commitment.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10. Emphasize two slots and the fixed-commitment rule. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Contrast a review point with a delivery promise. Check the X/Y/Z exchange. Read the selected pair and deferred item aloud in order.
Partner: Nora: Product owner / Samir: Commercial sponsor
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: There are two slots. X protects a fixed commitment; Y supports the agreed activation goal; Z has neither supplied basis. Each needs one slot, and splitting is infeasible. The rule prioritizes commitments, then activation.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Samir: Please record the rationale for every sponsor. Different informal explanations could make a consistent decision look arbitrary.
Nora: I will update the decision log with B and A, the capacity limit, and the review point for C. Your concern and the relevant evidence request will be recorded too.
Samir: Then I will tell the commercial team that the refresh is deferred under the agreed rule, not canceled forever, and that the next discussion needs evidence rather than another urgency label.
Nora: That keeps the product roadmap honest. We can pursue the current goal while leaving a clear route to reconsider the deferred request when the facts change.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.
