03User Stories and Acceptance CriteriaQuickly needs a clock and a test condition+

Your goal

Turn a vague performance request into a checkable criterion and distinguish that criterion from the wider completion standard.

This lesson in the book, page 22 ↗

A Read the situation

Quickly needs a clock and a test condition

Product manager Eva and test lead Omar refine a story saying an authorized editor should open the records page quickly. They agree a fictional acceptance test: 100 navigations with the fixed 1,000-record dataset and test profile N, timed from the navigation click until records and the primary action are usable. At least 95 runs must finish within 2 seconds, and every run within 5 seconds without an error. A trial records 96 within 2 seconds and four more within 4.5 seconds, with no errors. Other completion checks remain open.

Eva: Product manager / Omar: Test lead

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. When does the stated timing end?

Answer and explanation

D. When records and the primary action are usable

The criterion ends at usable content and action, not merely the start of technical activity.

2. Does the trial meet the stated timing and error criterion?

Answer and explanation

D. Yes; 96 finish within 2 seconds and all finish within 5 seconds without errors.

The supplied rule requires at least 95 fast runs and all runs within the five-second ceiling, with no errors.

3. What remains open after this trial?

Answer and explanation

B. Other completion checks

Passing this criterion does not complete the other checks explicitly left open in the briefing.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The empirical rule here is at least 95 of 100 runs within 2 seconds, with every run within 5 seconds and no errors. It is not an average-time target. A different percentile calculation convention should not silently replace the agreed count-based test.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
user story
A short description of user, capability, and intended benefit.refine the user story
acceptance criterion
A checkable condition for accepting a specific item.define acceptance criteria
Definition of Done
The agreed quality standard for a completed increment in Scrum.meet the Definition of Done
functional requirement
A required behavior or capability.specify a functional requirement
nonfunctional requirement
A required quality or constraint, such as performance.clarify a nonfunctional requirement
precondition
A state that must exist before the test or action.state the precondition
postcondition
A state expected after the action completes.verify the postcondition
test fixture
Controlled data and setup used for a repeatable test.prepare the test fixture
test profile
The defined environment and conditions for a test.use the agreed test profile
navigation event
The action that initiates movement to a page or view.timestamp the navigation event
usable state
The condition in which the intended task can actually be performed.define the usable state
latency
Time between a defined initiating event and response.measure latency
response-time threshold
A specified boundary for an acceptable duration.set the response-time threshold
percentile
A position in a distribution defined by a proportion of observations.report the percentile basis
tail latency
Response times in the slower part of a distribution.monitor tail latency
timeout
A defined limit after which an operation stops waiting.specify timeout behavior
error state
A condition showing that the requested operation did not complete as intended.handle the error state
empty state
The interface shown when no relevant content is available.design the empty state
edge case
An unusual or limiting situation that requires defined behavior.cover edge cases
happy path
The intended successful sequence without exceptional conditions.test the happy path
testability
The ability to evaluate a requirement with a defined check.improve testability
ambiguity
Wording that allows conflicting interpretations.remove ambiguity
acceptance test
A test evaluating agreed acceptance conditions.run the acceptance test
completion evidence
Records supporting a claim that required work is complete.attach completion evidence

The trial passes this criterion: 96 exceeds the required 95, and the four slower runs still finish before 5 seconds. The evidence applies to the fixed dataset and profile N. Other environments and the remaining completion requirements are not established by this result.

C Notice the language

Define completion together

A product manager can specify the user outcome without prescribing every engineering detail. A test lead can make it measurable without replacing that outcome with a convenient technical proxy. Agree the user state, timing boundaries, test basis, and pass rule in language both can defend.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Clarify the user state
The story concerns an authorized editor.
Replace the adjective
Quickly needs a measurable timing rule.
Define the start
Start the clock at the navigation click.
Define the finish
Stop when the records and primary action are usable.
Fix the test data
Use the agreed 1,000-record dataset.
Name the environment
Run under test profile N.
State the main threshold
At least 95 of 100 runs must finish within 2 seconds.
Bound the slow runs
Every run must finish within 5 seconds.
Include failure behavior
The test must complete without an error.
Avoid a convenient proxy
A visible loading shell is not a usable records page.
Distinguish statistics
An average does not establish the required run count.
Report the evidence
Ninety-six runs met 2 seconds; all met the ceiling.
Keep the scope
This result applies to the defined test conditions.
Separate completion layers
This acceptance criterion does not replace the Definition of Done.
Preserve open work
The remaining completion checks are still open.
Close the story carefully
Attach the evidence before changing the completion status.
6 language and precision notes
Quickly
A preference until the measurement and limit are defined.
Loaded
Could mean visual, network, or usable completion; specify it.
Within
Includes the stated boundary unless the agreed rule says otherwise.
Average
Does not describe every run or the slow tail.
Accepted
Specify which criterion or work item was accepted.
Done
Requires the applicable completion standard, not only one passing test.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which criterion matches the agreed performance rule?

Answer and explanation

A. At least 95 of 100 defined navigations usable within 2 seconds, all within 5, without errors

The correct criterion preserves usable completion, both count and ceiling requirements, and the no-error condition. An average or a loading shell measures something different.

2. What if only 94 runs meet 2 seconds, while all finish before 5 seconds without errors?

Answer and explanation

A. The stated criterion fails.

The rule independently requires at least 95 runs within two seconds, so meeting the ceiling alone is insufficient.

3. Why time until the primary action is usable?

Answer and explanation

A. The user must be able to perform the intended task, not merely see a loading shell.

The defined endpoint connects performance evidence to usable behavior instead of an earlier technical event.

4. What can Eva say after the actual trial?

Answer and explanation

C. This criterion passed under profile N; other completion checks remain open.

The evidence supports the stated test result, not untested conditions or all remaining work.

Check explanations in the book, page 89 ↗

E Conversations

Quickly needs a clock and a test condition

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Eva: Product manager / Omar: Test lead

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • usable state
  • Tail latency
  • happy path
  • acceptance test
  • test profile
  • Definition of Done
  • response-time threshold
  • acceptance criterion
  • completion evidence
  • test fixture
  1. Eva
    The story says an authorized editor should open the records page quickly. Engineering asked what quickly means, and I can see that we have left too much room for interpretation.
  2. Omar
    Let us define an 1 tied to the task. What event starts the timing, and what must be usable before we say the page has finished loading?
  3. Eva
    Start at the navigation click. Finish when the records are available and the primary action works, not when the user sees only a loading shell.
  4. Omar
    That defines the 2. A visible placeholder is not enough because the editor still cannot perform the intended task at that point.
  5. Eva
    We should fix the data as well. Let us use the agreed one-thousand-record dataset so a smaller empty page does not stand in for the actual check.
  6. Omar
    Use that 3 with profile N. The evidence needs its configuration attached so another reviewer can understand what was measured and repeat the check appropriately.
  7. Eva
    For the main threshold, I propose that at least ninety-five of one hundred navigations finish within two seconds. We should also define what happens to the slower runs.
  8. Omar
    Add a 4 of five seconds for every run, with no errors. That prevents a good fast-run count from concealing an unacceptable slow tail.
  9. Eva
    The dashboard gives me an average. Could you send the individual run counts as well? Otherwise I cannot check our ninety-five-out-of-a-hundred rule.
  10. Omar
    Correct. 5 matters to the experience too. Keep the exact rule in the story rather than replace it with a different summary statistic during reporting.
  11. Eva
    The trial produced ninety-six runs within two seconds. The other four finished within four point five seconds, and there were no errors.
  12. Omar
    Then this 6 passes on the supplied result. Ninety-six meets the minimum, and all one hundred remain within the separate ceiling without an error.
  13. Eva
    I can report that clearly, but I should not imply that every device, network, or dataset has been tested. The result belongs to the agreed setup.
  14. Omar
    Keep the 7 visible. The conclusion applies to profile N and the defined data, not automatically to every environment a future user might have.
  15. Eva
    Can I mark the performance criterion passed and leave the story open? We still have completion checks outstanding, and I do not want to hide them.
  16. Omar
    Exactly. The 8 is a separate completion standard where the team uses Scrum. One story-specific criterion does not replace the agreed quality requirements for the increment.
  17. Eva
    We should preserve the distinction between an ordinary successful path and the other states the product must handle. This test did not define every exceptional situation.
  18. Omar
    Yes. The 9 is not the entire behavior of the product. Keep the remaining checks visible rather than let one useful test stand for everything.
  19. Eva
    I will attach the results, keep the unresolved checks open, and use the exact criterion in the next review. That should end the debate over what quickly means here.
  20. Omar
    The 10 will then support a precise status. We have a measurable requirement and a passing result within scope, without quietly changing the meaning of done.

Answers and explanations
  1. acceptance criterion Turn 2. An acceptance criterion turns the vague performance request into a specific condition that can be evaluated.
  2. usable state Turn 4. The usable state requires the records and primary action to be available, avoiding an earlier but misleading loading milestone.
  3. test fixture Turn 6. The test fixture supplies controlled data and setup, here the fixed one-thousand-record dataset.
  4. response-time threshold Turn 8. The response-time threshold defines an acceptable duration; the case also sets a separate ceiling for every run.
  5. Tail latency Turn 10. Tail latency concerns the slower responses that can be hidden by an average and are bounded here by the five-second ceiling.
  6. acceptance test Turn 12. The acceptance test passes because the recorded run counts, ceiling, and no-error condition all satisfy the agreed rule.
  7. test profile Turn 14. The test profile identifies the environment and conditions under which the passing result was obtained.
  8. Definition of Done Turn 16. The Definition of Done is the wider agreed quality standard in Scrum, not a substitute label for one story-specific passing criterion.
  9. happy path Turn 18. The happy path is the intended successful sequence; covering it does not automatically address all exceptional or empty states.
  10. completion evidence Turn 20. Completion evidence records what has actually passed and what remains outstanding, supporting an accurate completion decision.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Eva: The story says an authorized editor should open the records page quickly. Engineering asked what quickly means, and I can see that we have left too much room for interpretation.
  2. Omar: Let us define an acceptance criterion tied to the task. What event starts the timing, and what must be usable before we say the page has finished loading?
  3. Eva: Start at the navigation click. Finish when the records are available and the primary action works, not when the user sees only a loading shell.
  4. Omar: That defines the usable state. A visible placeholder is not enough because the editor still cannot perform the intended task at that point.
  5. Eva: We should fix the data as well. Let us use the agreed one-thousand-record dataset so a smaller empty page does not stand in for the actual check.
  6. Omar: Use that test fixture with profile N. The evidence needs its configuration attached so another reviewer can understand what was measured and repeat the check appropriately.
  7. Eva: For the main threshold, I propose that at least ninety-five of one hundred navigations finish within two seconds. We should also define what happens to the slower runs.
  8. Omar: Add a response-time threshold of five seconds for every run, with no errors. That prevents a good fast-run count from concealing an unacceptable slow tail.
  9. Eva: The dashboard gives me an average. Could you send the individual run counts as well? Otherwise I cannot check our ninety-five-out-of-a-hundred rule.
  10. Omar: Correct. Tail latency matters to the experience too. Keep the exact rule in the story rather than replace it with a different summary statistic during reporting.
  11. Eva: The trial produced ninety-six runs within two seconds. The other four finished within four point five seconds, and there were no errors.
  12. Omar: Then this acceptance test passes on the supplied result. Ninety-six meets the minimum, and all one hundred remain within the separate ceiling without an error.
  13. Eva: I can report that clearly, but I should not imply that every device, network, or dataset has been tested. The result belongs to the agreed setup.
  14. Omar: Keep the test profile visible. The conclusion applies to profile N and the defined data, not automatically to every environment a future user might have.
  15. Eva: Can I mark the performance criterion passed and leave the story open? We still have completion checks outstanding, and I do not want to hide them.
  16. Omar: Exactly. The Definition of Done is a separate completion standard where the team uses Scrum. One story-specific criterion does not replace the agreed quality requirements for the increment.
  17. Eva: We should preserve the distinction between an ordinary successful path and the other states the product must handle. This test did not define every exceptional situation.
  18. Omar: Yes. The happy path is not the entire behavior of the product. Keep the remaining checks visible rather than let one useful test stand for everything.
  19. Eva: I will attach the results, keep the unresolved checks open, and use the exact criterion in the next review. That should end the debate over what quickly means here.
  20. Omar: The completion evidence will then support a precise status. We have a measurable requirement and a passing result within scope, without quietly changing the meaning of done.
Dialogue in the book, page 28 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Make quickly testable

Fictional refinement using the lesson case: a user story says a page should load quickly without defining a measurable completion condition.

  1. Quality engineer: The user story says quickly. Could you specify what timing and conditions would count as acceptable?
  2. Product manager: I haven't agreed a threshold yet. We should clarify the user expectation before choosing a number.
  3. Quality engineer: Please include where timing starts and ends, plus the representative data and device conditions.
  4. Product manager: That will help us avoid a test that passes only because it measures a different interaction.
  5. Quality engineer: Exactly. Can we review proposed acceptance criteria before the team commits to the story?
  6. Product manager: Yes. I'll bring the user context and ask engineering for feasible measurement conditions at refinement.
  7. Quality engineer: Should the definition of done replace those story-specific criteria if it already requires testing?
  8. Product manager: No. The shared completion checklist doesn't define what quickly means for this particular page.
  9. Quality engineer: I'll keep the timing criterion open and identify empty and large-data cases for discussion.
  10. Product manager: Good. We can agree a testable boundary without inventing a performance promise today.
What should happen when there are no results?

Fictional search-story review: the story describes displaying matching records but says nothing about a search with zero matches.

  1. Frontend developer: The story covers matching records. What should users see when the search returns no results?
  2. Product owner: That edge case isn't defined. We need a deliberate empty-state behavior rather than an accidental blank panel.
  3. Frontend developer: Could you clarify whether the search term stays visible so users can revise it?
  4. Product owner: I propose keeping it visible, but let's confirm the interaction with design before treating that as approved.
  5. Frontend developer: Please bring the proposed behavior to tomorrow's refinement so I can estimate the complete story.
  6. Product owner: I'll include the no-results case in the acceptance-criteria discussion and ask quality to review it.
  7. Frontend developer: Should an unavailable search service show the same empty state because both return no displayed records?
  8. Product owner: No, those are distinct situations to specify. Missing matches shouldn't be confused with a failed search.
  9. Frontend developer: I'll identify both cases and leave their final behavior pending the refinement decision.
  10. Product owner: Then we'll have explicit criteria instead of discovering the missing distinction during acceptance testing.
Saved locally or shared with the team?

Fictional story discussion: a requirement says save the filter, but does not specify whether it belongs to one user or the whole team.

  1. Backend developer: When this story says save the filter, whose future sessions should use that saved setting?
  2. Product owner: I intended a personal filter, but the story doesn't make that ownership clear.
  3. Backend developer: Please confirm whether personal means this device only or the user's account across devices.
  4. Product owner: That remains undecided. I'll ask the product designer to clarify the intended use case with me.
  5. Backend developer: Could we resolve it before estimation? The persistence and access expectations affect the implementation scope.
  6. Product owner: Yes. I'll request that decision at refinement and include the resulting acceptance criteria.
  7. Backend developer: We should also discuss the edge case where a saved filter refers to a removed category.
  8. Product owner: Agreed. Let's define that behavior explicitly instead of assuming the general definition of done covers it.
  9. Backend developer: I'll bring the technical questions, but I won't silently choose account-wide storage as the product requirement.
  10. Product owner: Thank you. We'll close refinement with the ownership boundary and any remaining decision clearly recorded.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Quickly needs a clock and a test condition
  1. Read turns 1-10. Stress usable, at least 95, and every run.
  2. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Keep criterion passed distinct from story complete.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

A ceiling does not replace the main rule

A test requires at least 95 of 100 runs within 2 seconds and all within 5 seconds without errors. The result is 93 within 2 seconds; the remaining seven finish within 4 seconds without errors.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Tester: "The fast-run count is ___."

Answer and explanation

C. 93

The result records ninety-three runs within the two-second threshold.

2. Product: "The required fast-run count is at least ___."

Answer and explanation

A. 95

The acceptance rule requires at least ninety-five runs within two seconds.

3. Tester: "The overall criterion ___."

Answer and explanation

C. fails

The fast-run requirement is missed even though the five-second ceiling and error condition are met.

4. Product: "The recorded timing unit is ___."

Answer and explanation

D. seconds

All thresholds and observed durations in the briefing are expressed in seconds.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Lesson 03: User Stories and Acceptance Criteria
Original fictional case: Product manager Eva and test lead Omar refine a story saying an authorized editor should open the records page quickly. They agree a fictional acceptance test: 100 navigations with the fixed 1,000-record dataset and test profile N, timed from the navigation click until records and the primary action are usable. At least 95 runs must finish within 2 seconds, and every run within 5 seconds without an error. A trial records 96 within 2 seconds and four more within 4.5 seconds, with no errors. Other completion checks remain open.
Language workshop: Quickly needs a clock and a test condition
Communication goal: Turn a vague performance request into a checkable criterion and distinguish that criterion from the wider completion standard.
Language guidance: The empirical rule here is at least 95 of 100 runs within 2 seconds, with every run within 5 seconds and no errors. It is not an average-time target. A different percentile calculation convention should not silently replace the agreed count-based test.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The story concerns an authorized editor. / Quickly needs a measurable timing rule. / Start the clock at the navigation click. / Stop when the records and primary action are usable. / Use the agreed 1,000-record dataset. / Run under test profile N. / At least 95 of 100 runs must finish within 2 seconds. / Every run must finish within 5 seconds. / The test must complete without an error. / A visible loading shell is not a usable records page. / An average does not establish the required run count. / Ninety-six runs met 2 seconds; all met the ceiling. / This result applies to the defined test conditions. / This acceptance criterion does not replace the Definition of Done. / The remaining completion checks are still open. / Attach the evidence before changing the completion status.
Vocabulary: user story: A short description of user, capability, and intended benefit. / acceptance criterion: A checkable condition for accepting a specific item. / Definition of Done: The agreed quality standard for a completed increment in Scrum. / functional requirement: A required behavior or capability. / nonfunctional requirement: A required quality or constraint, such as performance. / precondition: A state that must exist before the test or action. / postcondition: A state expected after the action completes. / test fixture: Controlled data and setup used for a repeatable test. / test profile: The defined environment and conditions for a test. / navigation event: The action that initiates movement to a page or view. / usable state: The condition in which the intended task can actually be performed. / latency: Time between a defined initiating event and response. / response-time threshold: A specified boundary for an acceptable duration. / percentile: A position in a distribution defined by a proportion of observations. / tail latency: Response times in the slower part of a distribution. / timeout: A defined limit after which an operation stops waiting. / error state: A condition showing that the requested operation did not complete as intended. / empty state: The interface shown when no relevant content is available. / edge case: An unusual or limiting situation that requires defined behavior. / happy path: The intended successful sequence without exceptional conditions. / testability: The ability to evaluate a requirement with a defined check. / ambiguity: Wording that allows conflicting interpretations. / acceptance test: A test evaluating agreed acceptance conditions. / completion evidence: Records supporting a claim that required work is complete.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

04Metrics, Funnels, and Product AnalyticsMore signups, fewer completed setups+

Your goal

Explain opposing funnel movements using aligned cohorts, counts, rates, and an explicit limit on causal interpretation.

This lesson in the book, page 31 ↗

A Read the situation

More signups, fewer completed setups

Analyst Imani and growth lead Alex compare two fully matured signup cohorts using the same seven-day setup-completion definition. The earlier cohort has 1,000 signups and 600 completions; the later cohort has 1,500 signups and 500 completions. Event counts are verified. A campaign ran during the later period, but other conditions were not controlled, and no causal attribution is established. Alex calls the campaign a complete success. Imani must explain acquisition growth alongside the decline in completed setups and completion rate.

Alex: Growth lead / Imani: Product analyst

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. How did signup count change?

Answer and explanation

A. It rose from 1,000 to 1,500, a 50% increase.

Five hundred additional signups divided by the original thousand gives a fifty-percent increase.

2. How did completed setup count change?

Answer and explanation

D. It fell from 600 to 500.

The supplied completion counts show one hundred fewer completed setups in the later cohort.

3. What makes the time windows comparable here?

Answer and explanation

B. Both cohorts have completed the same seven-day observation window.

The briefing explicitly says both cohorts are fully matured under the same seven-day definition.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Signups rose 50%, but completed setups fell by 100, about 16.67% relative to 600. Completion rates fell from 60% to about 33.33%, a decline of about 26.67 percentage points. Neither the count nor the rate alone describes the entire funnel.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
funnel
A defined sequence of steps toward a product outcome.analyze the funnel
signup
A recorded account-registration event under the stated definition.count valid signups
activation
A defined early milestone indicating initial product value.define activation
setup completion
Completion of the specified initial configuration steps.measure setup completion
conversion rate
The share of an eligible population completing a defined action.calculate conversion rate
cohort
A group sharing a defined starting event or characteristic.compare signup cohorts
observation window
The period during which outcomes are counted.align the observation window
mature cohort
A cohort whose required observation period has elapsed.use mature cohorts
event definition
The rules specifying when an analytics event is recorded.maintain event definitions
instrumentation
The implementation used to collect product events and measurements.verify instrumentation
event property
A recorded attribute attached to an event.validate event properties
unique user
A distinct user under the stated identity rule.count unique users
deduplication
Removing repeated records of the same defined event or entity.apply deduplication
denominator
The eligible total used to calculate a rate.verify the denominator
drop-off
Loss of users between defined funnel steps.locate funnel drop-off
cohort maturity
The extent to which a cohort has completed its observation period.check cohort maturity
segmentation
Dividing data into relevant user or context groups.segment by acquisition channel
channel mix
The composition of traffic from different acquisition sources.examine channel mix
attribution
Assignment of credit for an outcome under a stated method.qualify campaign attribution
retention
Continued use or return under a defined time and activity rule.measure retention
leading indicator
A measure expected to precede a later outcome.validate a leading indicator
lagging indicator
A measure reflecting an outcome after relevant activity occurs.report a lagging indicator
guardrail metric
A measure used to detect unacceptable side effects.monitor guardrail metrics
metric contract
An agreed definition of a metric's event, population, and calculation.document the metric contract

Both seven-day windows are complete, so the later cohort is not simply younger in this case. The numbers are verified, but their causes remain unestablished. Channel mix, product friction, and other explanations need investigation rather than being asserted from the two totals.

C Notice the language

Report the whole defined outcome

Different teams may optimize different stages of a funnel. Acknowledge the stage that improved, then show what happened at the next meaningful outcome. Use aligned time windows and denominators so a discussion about success does not become a contest between selectively chosen metrics.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge acquisition
Signups increased from 1,000 to 1,500.
State the next outcome
Completed setups fell from 600 to 500.
Give the earlier rate
The earlier completion rate was 60%.
Give the later rate
The later completion rate was approximately 33.33%.
Name the rate difference
That is a decline of approximately 26.67 percentage points.
Keep counts visible
We have 100 fewer completed setups despite more signups.
Confirm timing
Both cohorts have a full seven-day window.
Confirm definitions
The setup-completion definition is unchanged.
Avoid a blanket success label
Acquisition improved, but the downstream outcome worsened.
Separate evidence from cause
The comparison does not isolate the campaign's effect.
Ask about the mix
Did the composition of acquisition channels change?
Locate the friction
Which setup step accounts for the observed drop-off?
Protect the denominator
Use eligible signups from the same cohort.
Avoid immature comparisons
Do not compare a one-day cohort with a complete seven-day cohort.
Define the next analysis
Segment the funnel and check the relevant step-level evidence.
Close the update
Report acquisition, completion count, completion rate, and the causal limitation.
6 language and precision notes
Success
Specify the objective and stage used to judge it.
Conversion
Needs a starting population, event, and time window.
Users
Define identity and avoid counting repeated events as people.
Drop-off
Locate the step; a total alone does not identify the obstacle.
Attributed
Depends on a method and is not automatically causal proof.
Mature
Describes observation time, not product-market maturity.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which summary represents both movements?

Answer and explanation

D. Signups increased, while completed setups and the setup-completion rate declined.

The supplied counts show acquisition growth together with fewer completions and a lower rate.

2. What is the later setup-completion rate?

Answer and explanation

A. Approximately 33.33%

Five hundred completions divided by fifteen hundred signups equals one third.

3. What is the approximate rate decline in percentage points?

Answer and explanation

D. 26.67

The earlier sixty-percent rate minus approximately thirty-three point three three percent is about 26.67 points.

4. Which causal statement is supported?

Answer and explanation

C. The campaign's causal effect is not isolated by this comparison.

Verified observations do not remove the uncontrolled differences between the two periods.

E Conversations

More signups, fewer completed setups

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Alex: Growth lead / Imani: Product analyst

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • denominator
  • conversion rate
  • channel mix
  • attribution
  • segmentation
  • funnel
  • drop-off
  • mature cohort
  • setup completion
  • instrumentation
  1. Alex
    Signups increased from one thousand to fifteen hundred during the campaign period. I called that a complete success in the update, but you flagged the setup numbers.
  2. Imani
    The 1 shows a mixed result. Completed setups fell from six hundred to five hundred, even though more people entered at the signup stage.
  3. Alex
    I want to make sure the later group has had enough time. Comparing a new cohort with an older one could make completion look worse simply because users are still setting up.
  4. Imani
    Each is a 2 in this comparison. Both have a full seven-day window, and the setup-completion definition is the same in both periods.
  5. Alex
    Then the counts describe a real difference in the recorded outcome, not merely an incomplete observation window. We gained five hundred signups but lost one hundred completed setups.
  6. Imani
    Correct. Keep the 3 with each rate. The earlier rate is six hundred divided by one thousand, while the later is five hundred divided by fifteen hundred.
  7. Alex
    That is sixty percent before and about thirty-three point three three percent later. The decline in the rate is larger than the decline in the completion count.
  8. Imani
    Yes. The 4 fell by about twenty-six point six seven percentage points. The count and the rate answer related but different questions.
  9. Alex
    I have sixteen point six seven percent in my slide. That is the fall in completed setups, not the change in conversion rate. I need separate labels.
  10. Imani
    Exactly. State 5 in both forms when needed: five hundred completions and roughly thirty-three percent of signups. Neither should disappear behind the favorable acquisition headline.
  11. Alex
    Could the campaign have attracted people who were less ready to configure the product? That seems plausible, but we have not examined the composition of the new signups.
  12. Imani
    Investigate 6 and other relevant differences. Plausibility is a reason to analyze, not enough to assign the entire decline to the campaign.
  13. Alex
    Can we break setup into steps for the next review? I can see the loss in the total, but not where people got stuck.
  14. Imani
    Locate the 7 using the appropriate step-level evidence. A product problem, a changed audience, and other explanations need different supporting records.
  15. Alex
    The event counts have been verified for this comparison. That removes one data-quality concern, but it does not make the business explanation automatic.
  16. Imani
    Correct. Verified 8 supports trust in the observation. It does not show which uncontrolled difference between the periods caused the outcome.
  17. Alex
    The next analysis can compare relevant channels and user groups while keeping the seven-day definition fixed. We should not change the metric halfway through to recover a favorable result.
  18. Imani
    Use 9 with a clear purpose and consistent definitions. It can reveal where the problem is concentrated without pretending that every descriptive split proves causality.
  19. Alex
    I will revise the update to say acquisition improved but the downstream completion outcome worsened. The campaign's overall contribution remains a question for analysis, not a settled success claim.
  20. Imani
    That qualifies 10 appropriately. The team can acknowledge the signup gain and address the completion decline using the same honest account of the evidence.

Answers and explanations
  1. funnel Turn 2. The funnel includes the later setup outcome as well as signup, so acquisition alone cannot represent the whole result.
  2. mature cohort Turn 4. A mature cohort has completed the defined observation period; both groups here have a full seven-day window.
  3. denominator Turn 6. The denominator is each cohort's eligible signup count, which must remain aligned with its own completions.
  4. conversion rate Turn 8. The conversion rate is the share of signups completing setup within seven days, not the raw completion count.
  5. setup completion Turn 10. Setup completion is the defined downstream event whose count and rate both declined in the supplied comparison.
  6. channel mix Turn 12. Channel mix concerns the composition of acquisition sources and is a possible explanation to investigate, not an established cause here.
  7. drop-off Turn 14. Drop-off identifies loss between defined funnel steps; step-level evidence is needed to locate the obstacle.
  8. instrumentation Turn 16. Instrumentation produces the event records; verifying those counts does not establish the causal explanation for their movement.
  9. segmentation Turn 18. Segmentation separates relevant groups for analysis while preserving the defined outcome and comparison basis.
  10. attribution Turn 20. Attribution assigns credit under a stated method; the uncontrolled cohort comparison does not isolate the campaign's causal contribution.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Alex: Signups increased from one thousand to fifteen hundred during the campaign period. I called that a complete success in the update, but you flagged the setup numbers.
  2. Imani: The funnel shows a mixed result. Completed setups fell from six hundred to five hundred, even though more people entered at the signup stage.
  3. Alex: I want to make sure the later group has had enough time. Comparing a new cohort with an older one could make completion look worse simply because users are still setting up.
  4. Imani: Each is a mature cohort in this comparison. Both have a full seven-day window, and the setup-completion definition is the same in both periods.
  5. Alex: Then the counts describe a real difference in the recorded outcome, not merely an incomplete observation window. We gained five hundred signups but lost one hundred completed setups.
  6. Imani: Correct. Keep the denominator with each rate. The earlier rate is six hundred divided by one thousand, while the later is five hundred divided by fifteen hundred.
  7. Alex: That is sixty percent before and about thirty-three point three three percent later. The decline in the rate is larger than the decline in the completion count.
  8. Imani: Yes. The conversion rate fell by about twenty-six point six seven percentage points. The count and the rate answer related but different questions.
  9. Alex: I have sixteen point six seven percent in my slide. That is the fall in completed setups, not the change in conversion rate. I need separate labels.
  10. Imani: Exactly. State setup completion in both forms when needed: five hundred completions and roughly thirty-three percent of signups. Neither should disappear behind the favorable acquisition headline.
  11. Alex: Could the campaign have attracted people who were less ready to configure the product? That seems plausible, but we have not examined the composition of the new signups.
  12. Imani: Investigate channel mix and other relevant differences. Plausibility is a reason to analyze, not enough to assign the entire decline to the campaign.
  13. Alex: Can we break setup into steps for the next review? I can see the loss in the total, but not where people got stuck.
  14. Imani: Locate the drop-off using the appropriate step-level evidence. A product problem, a changed audience, and other explanations need different supporting records.
  15. Alex: The event counts have been verified for this comparison. That removes one data-quality concern, but it does not make the business explanation automatic.
  16. Imani: Correct. Verified instrumentation supports trust in the observation. It does not show which uncontrolled difference between the periods caused the outcome.

Dialogue in the book, page 37 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

More sign-ups, fewer completed setups

Fictional analytics discussion using the lesson case: sign-ups increased but fewer users completed setup; a stakeholder calls the campaign a complete success.

  1. Marketing stakeholder: Sign-ups increased, so I called the acquisition campaign a complete product success in the update.
  2. Product analyst: Sign-ups improved, but fewer users completed setup. Those stages tell us different things.
  3. Marketing stakeholder: Could you explain activation without implying every sign-up should be counted as an active user?
  4. Product analyst: Here activation is reaching the defined setup milestone. Signing up enters the funnel but doesn't complete it.
  5. Marketing stakeholder: Then acquisition performance can improve while the next stage still raises a concern.
  6. Product analyst: Exactly. We should inspect consistent cohorts and definitions before attributing the setup change to the campaign.
  7. Marketing stakeholder: Does this evidence tell us whether those users will stay, or is retention another question?
  8. Product analyst: Retention is another question about continued use over time, not established by this setup count.
  9. Marketing stakeholder: I'll narrow the success claim to sign-ups and make the setup issue visible.
  10. Product analyst: I'll bring the funnel breakdown and any comparability gaps before we recommend a response.
A returning visit is not the defined retention event

Fictional product dashboard: retention is defined as completing a project task in a later week, but a draft counts any returning page view.

  1. Product manager: The draft retention chart counts returning page views. Is that our agreed retained-user definition?
  2. Product analyst: No. Our current definition requires completing a project task in a later week.
  3. Product manager: Then a visit and a retained user are not interchangeable measures for this report.
  4. Product analyst: Correct. A returning visit shows activity, but it doesn't establish the task-based milestone we agreed.
  5. Product manager: Could you explain that distinction on the chart without suggesting page views have no analytical value?
  6. Product analyst: I'll label them as visits and calculate retention separately using the defined event and cohort.
  7. Product manager: Do we know whether the required completion events are available for every week in the comparison?
  8. Product analyst: That coverage still needs checking. I'll flag missing periods rather than count them as zero retention.
  9. Product manager: Good. Keep the metric definition and observation window visible when you bring the revised view.
  10. Product analyst: Then readers can distinguish engagement activity from the specific retention measure we actually use.
A younger cohort has had less time

Fictional funnel review: an older sign-up cohort has seven days of setup observation; a newer cohort has only two days.

  1. Growth manager: The newer cohort has fewer completed setups. Does that prove the new onboarding flow performs worse?
  2. Product analyst: Not yet. The older cohort has seven observation days, while the newer one has only two.
  3. Growth manager: So we are comparing different opportunities to reach activation, not simply different interface versions.
  4. Product analyst: Exactly. A cohort groups users by a shared starting event, but the observation window still matters.
  5. Growth manager: Could you compare both cohorts at day two instead of waiting another five days?
  6. Product analyst: That could provide an early matched view if the required event history is available.
  7. Growth manager: And a day-two comparison wouldn't establish the seven-day result or long-term retention.
  8. Product analyst: Correct. We should label the chosen window and keep later outcomes separate.
  9. Growth manager: I'll remove the immediate failure claim and ask for the matched early funnel view.
  10. Product analyst: I'll check event coverage and bring the comparison with its timing boundary clearly stated.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

More signups, fewer completed setups
  1. Read turns 1-10. Keep signup counts and completion rates clearly separated.
  2. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Stress possible explanations without making them causes.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

A bigger entrance, a smaller outcome

Two mature seven-day cohorts use the same definition. The first has 200 signups and 100 setup completions. The second has 400 signups and 80 completions. The cause is not established.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Analyst: "The earlier completion rate was ___ percent."

Answer and explanation

B. 50

One hundred completions divided by two hundred signups equals fifty percent.

2. Growth: "The later rate was ___ percent."

Answer and explanation

B. 20

Eighty completions divided by four hundred signups equals twenty percent.

3. Analyst: "The rate declined by ___ percentage points."

Answer and explanation

C. 30

Fifty percent minus twenty percent equals thirty percentage points.

4. Growth: "The cause remains ___."

Answer and explanation

D. unestablished

The briefing supplies descriptive cohort results but no established causal explanation.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Lesson 04: Metrics, Funnels, and Product Analytics
Original fictional case: Analyst Imani and growth lead Alex compare two fully matured signup cohorts using the same seven-day setup-completion definition. The earlier cohort has 1,000 signups and 600 completions; the later cohort has 1,500 signups and 500 completions. Event counts are verified. A campaign ran during the later period, but other conditions were not controlled, and no causal attribution is established. Alex calls the campaign a complete success. Imani must explain acquisition growth alongside the decline in completed setups and completion rate.
Language workshop: More signups, fewer completed setups
Communication goal: Explain opposing funnel movements using aligned cohorts, counts, rates, and an explicit limit on causal interpretation.
Language guidance: Signups rose 50%, but completed setups fell by 100, about 16.67% relative to 600. Completion rates fell from 60% to about 33.33%, a decline of about 26.67 percentage points. Neither the count nor the rate alone describes the entire funnel.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Signups increased from 1,000 to 1,500. / Completed setups fell from 600 to 500. / The earlier completion rate was 60%. / The later completion rate was approximately 33.33%. / That is a decline of approximately 26.67 percentage points. / We have 100 fewer completed setups despite more signups. / Both cohorts have a full seven-day window. / The setup-completion definition is unchanged. / Acquisition improved, but the downstream outcome worsened. / The comparison does not isolate the campaign's effect. / Did the composition of acquisition channels change? / Which setup step accounts for the observed drop-off? / Use eligible signups from the same cohort. / Do not compare a one-day cohort with a complete seven-day cohort. / Segment the funnel and check the relevant step-level evidence. / Report acquisition, completion count, completion rate, and the causal limitation.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Course: Software Product Management English

Lesson 04: Metrics, Funnels, and Product Analytics
Original fictional case: Analyst Imani and growth lead Alex compare two fully matured signup cohorts using the same seven-day setup-completion definition. The earlier cohort has 1,000 signups and 600 completions; the later cohort has 1,500 signups and 500 completions. Event counts are verified. A campaign ran during the later period, but other conditions were not controlled, and no causal attribution is established. Alex calls the campaign a complete success. Imani must explain acquisition growth alongside the decline in completed setups and completion rate.
Language workshop: More signups, fewer completed setups
Communication goal: Explain opposing funnel movements using aligned cohorts, counts, rates, and an explicit limit on causal interpretation.
Language guidance: Signups rose 50%, but completed setups fell by 100, about 16.67% relative to 600. Completion rates fell from 60% to about 33.33%, a decline of about 26.67 percentage points. Neither the count nor the rate alone describes the entire funnel.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Signups increased from 1,000 to 1,500. / Completed setups fell from 600 to 500. / The earlier completion rate was 60%. / The later completion rate was approximately 33.33%. / That is a decline of approximately 26.67 percentage points. / We have 100 fewer completed setups despite more signups. / Both cohorts have a full seven-day window. / The setup-completion definition is unchanged. / Acquisition improved, but the downstream outcome worsened. / The comparison does not isolate the campaign's effect. / Did the composition of acquisition channels change? / Which setup step accounts for the observed drop-off? / Use eligible signups from the same cohort. / Do not compare a one-day cohort with a complete seven-day cohort. / Segment the funnel and check the relevant step-level evidence. / Report acquisition, completion count, completion rate, and the causal limitation.
Vocabulary: funnel: A defined sequence of steps toward a product outcome. / signup: A recorded account-registration event under the stated definition. / activation: A defined early milestone indicating initial product value. / setup completion: Completion of the specified initial configuration steps. / conversion rate: The share of an eligible population completing a defined action. / cohort: A group sharing a defined starting event or characteristic. / observation window: The period during which outcomes are counted. / mature cohort: A cohort whose required observation period has elapsed. / event definition: The rules specifying when an analytics event is recorded. / instrumentation: The implementation used to collect product events and measurements. / event property: A recorded attribute attached to an event. / unique user: A distinct user under the stated identity rule. / deduplication: Removing repeated records of the same defined event or entity. / denominator: The eligible total used to calculate a rate. / drop-off: Loss of users between defined funnel steps. / cohort maturity: The extent to which a cohort has completed its observation period. / segmentation: Dividing data into relevant user or context groups. / channel mix: The composition of traffic from different acquisition sources. / attribution: Assignment of credit for an outcome under a stated method. / retention: Continued use or return under a defined time and activity rule. / leading indicator: A measure expected to precede a later outcome. / lagging indicator: A measure reflecting an outcome after relevant activity occurs. / guardrail metric: A measure used to detect unacceptable side effects. / metric contract: An agreed definition of a metric's event, population, and calculation.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10. Keep signup counts and completion rates clearly separated. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Stress possible explanations without making them causes. Check the transfer. Read each rate and the percentage-point decline with its unit.
Partner: Alex: Growth lead / Imani: Product analyst
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Two mature seven-day cohorts use the same definition. The first has 200 signups and 100 setup completions. The second has 400 signups and 80 completions. The cause is not established.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Alex: The next analysis can compare relevant channels and user groups while keeping the seven-day definition fixed. We should not change the metric halfway through to recover a favorable result.
Imani: Use segmentation with a clear purpose and consistent definitions. It can reveal where the problem is concentrated without pretending that every descriptive split proves causality.
Alex: I will revise the update to say acquisition improved but the downstream completion outcome worsened. The campaign's overall contribution remains a question for analysis, not a settled success claim.
Imani: That qualifies attribution appropriately. The team can acknowledge the signup gain and address the completion decline using the same honest account of the evidence.
END REFERENCE
05Experimentation and A/B TestingA day-one lead is not a final winner+

Your goal

Discuss interim experiment results without changing the decision rule after seeing favorable data, while preserving guardrail monitoring.

This lesson in the book, page 40 ↗

A Read the situation

A day-one lead is not a final winner

Product manager Jules and experimentation analyst Priya review day one of a randomized test. Control A has 100 conversions from 1,000 eligible users; variant B has 120 from 1,000. The pre-agreed fixed-horizon plan requires 14 days and at least 10,000 eligible users per arm before the final analysis. No sequential early-success rule is approved. The safety guardrail pauses an arm if its defined error rate exceeds 3%; current B errors are 1.2%. Data-quality checks are clear so far. Jules wants to announce B as the winner.

Jules: Product manager / Priya: Experimentation analyst

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What are the observed conversion rates?

Answer and explanation

C. A is 10% and B is 12%.

One hundred and one hundred twenty conversions divided by one thousand users give ten and twelve percent.

2. Which final-analysis prerequisites remain unmet?

Answer and explanation

A. Fourteen days and at least 10,000 users per arm

The case is on day one with one thousand users per arm, below both planned prerequisites.

3. Does B currently exceed the specified error guardrail?

Answer and explanation

A. No; 1.2% is below 3%.

The current error rate is below the stated threshold, which still remains active during the test.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The observed difference is 2 percentage points, or a 20% relative increase from the 10% control rate. These are descriptive day-one figures. No final significance conclusion is supplied, and the pre-agreed time and sample requirements are not met.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
A/B test
A randomized comparison between defined alternatives.run an A/B test
control arm
The experiment group receiving the reference experience.define the control arm
treatment arm
The group receiving the changed experience.monitor the treatment arm
random assignment
Allocation to groups by a defined random process.verify random assignment
unit of randomization
The entity assigned to an experiment group, such as a user.define the unit of randomization
eligibility rule
A condition determining which entities enter the test or analysis.apply eligibility rules
exposure event
The recorded event indicating encounter with the tested experience.validate the exposure event
primary metric
The main preselected measure for the experiment decision.specify the primary metric
guardrail
A predefined boundary protecting against unacceptable effects.monitor the guardrail
fixed-horizon test
A test analyzed under a preplanned endpoint rather than opportunistic stopping.follow the fixed-horizon plan
sequential testing
A statistical approach designed for valid repeated decision opportunities.use an approved sequential method
stopping rule
A predefined condition for ending or pausing an experiment.follow the stopping rule
minimum sample
The smallest planned analysis population on the stated basis.reach the minimum sample
minimum detectable effect
The effect size a planned test is designed to detect at stated power.specify the minimum detectable effect
statistical power
The probability of detecting a specified effect under the test assumptions.assess statistical power
confidence interval
An interval produced by a method with stated coverage properties.report a confidence interval
p-value
A probability under the null model of results at least as extreme as observed.interpret the p-value carefully
false positive
An erroneous rejection of a true null hypothesis under the stated test.control false-positive risk
multiple testing
Conducting more than one statistical comparison or decision opportunity.account for multiple testing
sample ratio mismatch
A difference from expected group allocation large enough to flag a validity concern.investigate sample ratio mismatch
novelty effect
A temporary response associated with encountering something new.assess novelty effects
day-of-week effect
Variation associated with the weekday composition of observations.examine day-of-week effects
interim result
A result observed before the planned final decision point.qualify interim results
practical significance
The operational importance of an effect, separate from statistical evidence.assess practical significance

A fixed-horizon success decision differs from an active guardrail response. The supplied guardrail is not breached at 1.2%, but it must still be monitored. Valid sequential methods can support different rules; none is approved for early success in this case.

C Notice the language

Do not rewrite the finish line after seeing the score

Interim data can support monitoring and investigation without establishing a final decision. State the planned analysis method and the difference between an early-success claim and a guardrail response. Waiting for planned evidence must never be confused with ignoring harm or a data-quality failure.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Give the counts
A has 100 conversions and B has 120, each from 1,000 users.
State the rates
The observed rates are 10% and 12%.
Separate effect expressions
The difference is two percentage points, or 20% relative.
Qualify maturity
This is a day-one result.
Recall the plan
Final analysis requires fourteen days and the planned sample.
Keep both prerequisites
The minimum is 10,000 eligible users per arm.
Reject a premature headline
B is ahead in the observed data, not yet a final winner.
Avoid opportunistic stopping
We have no approved early-success rule.
Preserve monitoring
Continue checking guardrails and data quality throughout.
Apply the actual threshold
The current 1.2% error rate is below the 3% guardrail.
Distinguish decisions
A harm-based pause is different from declaring success.
Ask about validity
Have allocation and event checks identified a problem?
Avoid invented certainty
The counts alone do not supply a final significance conclusion.
Consider representativeness
One day may not reflect the planned weekday mix.
State the next readout
Report the interim figures with the remaining evidence requirements.
Close the decision
Keep the final rollout decision pending the agreed analysis.
6 language and precision notes
Winner
A decision label that needs the planned evidence and rule.
Lift
Specify absolute percentage points or relative percent.
Peeking
Repeated inspection becomes a statistical issue when it changes decision behavior without an appropriate method.
Guardrail
Remains active during the test, not only at its endpoint.
Significant
Distinguish statistical evidence from practical importance.
Minimum
A requirement to meet, not a target to quietly lower after favorable data.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which update is justified?

Answer and explanation

C. B leads descriptively on day one; the planned final-analysis requirements are unmet.

The update preserves the observed lead without converting interim counts into a final decision.

2. What is the relative increase from 10% to 12%?

Answer and explanation

D. 20%

The two-point increase divided by the ten-percent control rate equals twenty percent.

3. What should happen if the defined guardrail is later exceeded?

Answer and explanation

A. Follow the pre-agreed pause and investigation rule.

The guardrail protects against unacceptable effects throughout the test and is distinct from the success analysis.

4. Why not stop for success simply because B leads today?

Answer and explanation

D. The fixed-horizon plan has no approved early-success method.

The correct decision follows the approved method; interim monitoring does not authorize opportunistic success stopping.

    Correct, and keep the 2 clear. Those expressions describe the observed difference; neither one supplies a final significance conclusion or a guarantee of rollout benefit.

    That changes the 4. Repeated opportunities to declare success need an appropriate statistical method, not an improvised rule added after seeing the data.

    If that error rate later crosses the threshold, we follow the pause and investigation process. The fixed horizon does not require us to ignore that change.
  12. Priya
    Correct. 6 could support different success rules if properly planned, but it is not the method approved here. Guardrail monitoring is still part of this plan.

  16. Priya

  18. Priya

Answers and explanations
  1. interim result Turn 2. An interim result is observed before the planned endpoint; these day-one counts do not establish the final experiment decision.
  2. control arm Turn 4. The control arm is A, whose ten-percent rate provides the reference for the stated relative increase.
  3. fixed-horizon test Turn 6. A fixed-horizon test follows its preplanned endpoint and analysis rules rather than stopping whenever an interim result looks favorable.
  4. stopping rule Turn 8. The stopping rule determines when a decision is permitted; changing it after repeated favorable looks can invalidate the planned inference.
  5. guardrail Turn 10. The guardrail protects against unacceptable effects during the experiment and remains active before the final success analysis.
  6. Sequential testing Turn 12. Sequential testing is a method designed for valid repeated decisions; none is approved here for declaring early success.
  7. sample ratio mismatch Turn 14. A sample ratio mismatch can indicate a problem in allocation or data capture, so it must be assessed rather than ignored for a favorable outcome.
  8. novelty effect Turn 16. A novelty effect is a temporary response to the new experience and is one possible reason early behavior may not persist.
  9. practical significance Turn 18. Practical significance concerns whether the effect matters operationally and is distinct from the statistical evidence for a difference.
  10. minimum sample Turn 20. The minimum sample is ten thousand eligible users per arm, an unmet prerequisite that cannot be silently lowered after favorable results.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

One day of lift is not a completed test

Fictional A/B-test review using the lesson case: one version has higher conversion after one day, before the planned duration ends.

  1. Growth lead: Version B has higher conversion after one day. Can we end the A/B test and launch it?
  2. Experiment analyst: The planned duration hasn't elapsed. The observed difference isn't yet the completed readout we agreed to use.
  3. Growth lead: Are you saying the higher rate is false, or that our conclusion should stay provisional?
  4. Experiment analyst: The observed rate stands. The interpretation needs the planned analysis, including uncertainty and guardrail metrics.
  5. Growth lead: Could statistical significance alone settle the rollout decision if a dashboard happens to display it today?
  6. Experiment analyst: It wouldn't replace the decision plan or review of practical effects and possible harms.
  7. Growth lead: Then I'll report early directional results without describing the hypothesis as confirmed.
  8. Experiment analyst: Good. We'll continue following the agreed monitoring and decision process, including any established safety escalation.
  9. Growth lead: Please tell me what is observed now and what remains pending at the next check-in.
  10. Experiment analyst: I'll bring those separately so one encouraging day doesn't become an unsupported product conclusion.
Conversion improves while support contacts rise

Fictional experiment readout: the treatment shows higher conversion and more support contacts; uncertainty and the prespecified guardrail evaluation are pending.

  1. Product manager: The treatment converts more users. Can our recommendation focus on that improvement alone?
  2. Experiment owner: Support contacts also increased, and the guardrail evaluation is pending. That belongs in the same discussion.
  3. Product manager: Does the increase prove users were confused by the treatment, or is that an interpretation?
  4. Experiment owner: It is an interpretation. We observed more contacts, but haven't established their cause or composition.
  5. Product manager: Then please separate the contact count from the hypothesis about confusion in the readout.
  6. Experiment owner: I will. We'll also assess uncertainty and the predefined guardrail rather than invent a threshold afterward.
  7. Product manager: Could a conversion gain still be practically unattractive if support workload rises enough?
  8. Experiment owner: Potentially. That tradeoff needs the actual evidence and agreed decision criteria, not just one favorable metric.
  9. Product manager: I'll leave the rollout recommendation open until that combined assessment is available.
  10. Experiment owner: I'll bring the benefit, guardrail result, and unresolved attribution questions together for review.
The test changed two things at once

Fictional product experiment: version B changes both a headline and button placement; the observed conversion rate is higher than version A.

  1. Designer: Version B converted more users, so the new headline must be the reason.
  2. Experiment analyst: B also moved the button. The comparison doesn't isolate the headline from that layout change.
  3. Designer: Then our hypothesis about the headline alone isn't directly tested by this combined treatment.
  4. Experiment analyst: Correct. We can discuss the observed combination while keeping individual-component explanations unconfirmed.
  5. Designer: Would a statistically significant combined effect prove that both changes helped independently?
  6. Experiment analyst: No. It would not establish each component's contribution or rule out interactions between them.
  7. Designer: I'll describe B as the combined version and remove the headline-only claim from the review.
  8. Experiment analyst: Please keep the exact treatment description with the result so future readers know what changed.
  9. Designer: If we need the individual contribution, we'll propose a separate experiment rather than rewrite this one's history.
  10. Experiment analyst: Agreed. First complete the planned readout, including guardrails, before deciding what further question to test.

The arithmetic is not the decision rule

On day two of a fixed-horizon test, A converts 40 of 500 users and B converts 50 of 500. The final time and sample requirements are unmet, and no early-success rule is approved.

Lesson 05: Experimentation and A/B Testing
Original fictional case: Product manager Jules and experimentation analyst Priya review day one of a randomized test. Control A has 100 conversions from 1,000 eligible users; variant B has 120 from 1,000. The pre-agreed fixed-horizon plan requires 14 days and at least 10,000 eligible users per arm before the final analysis. No sequential early-success rule is approved. The safety guardrail pauses an arm if its defined error rate exceeds 3%; current B errors are 1.2%. Data-quality checks are clear so far. Jules wants to announce B as the winner.
Language workshop: A day-one lead is not a final winner
Communication goal: Discuss interim experiment results without changing the decision rule after seeing favorable data, while preserving guardrail monitoring.
Language guidance: The observed difference is 2 percentage points, or a 20% relative increase from the 10% control rate. These are descriptive day-one figures. No final significance conclusion is supplied, and the pre-agreed time and sample requirements are not met.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): A has 100 conversions and B has 120, each from 1,000 users. / The observed rates are 10% and 12%. / The difference is two percentage points, or 20% relative. / This is a day-one result. / Final analysis requires fourteen days and the planned sample. / The minimum is 10,000 eligible users per arm. / B is ahead in the observed data, not yet a final winner. / We have no approved early-success rule. / Continue checking guardrails and data quality throughout. / The current 1.2% error rate is below the 3% guardrail. / A harm-based pause is different from declaring success. / Have allocation and event checks identified a problem? / The counts alone do not supply a final significance conclusion. / One day may not reflect the planned weekday mix. / Report the interim figures with the remaining evidence requirements. / Keep the final rollout decision pending the agreed analysis.
Vocabulary: A/B test: A randomized comparison between defined alternatives. / control arm: The experiment group receiving the reference experience. / treatment arm: The group receiving the changed experience. / random assignment: Allocation to groups by a defined random process. / unit of randomization: The entity assigned to an experiment group, such as a user. / eligibility rule: A condition determining which entities enter the test or analysis. / exposure event: The recorded event indicating encounter with the tested experience. / primary metric: The main preselected measure for the experiment decision. / guardrail: A predefined boundary protecting against unacceptable effects. / fixed-horizon test: A test analyzed under a preplanned endpoint rather than opportunistic stopping. / sequential testing: A statistical approach designed for valid repeated decision opportunities. / stopping rule: A predefined condition for ending or pausing an experiment. / minimum sample: The smallest planned analysis population on the stated basis. / minimum detectable effect: The effect size a planned test is designed to detect at stated power. / statistical power: The probability of detecting a specified effect under the test assumptions. / confidence interval: An interval produced by a method with stated coverage properties. / p-value: A probability under the null model of results at least as extreme as observed. / false positive: An erroneous rejection of a true null hypothesis under the stated test. / multiple testing: Conducting more than one statistical comparison or decision opportunity. / sample ratio mismatch: A difference from expected group allocation large enough to flag a validity concern. / novelty effect: A temporary response associated with encountering something new. / day-of-week effect: Variation associated with the weekday composition of observations. / interim result: A result observed before the planned final decision point. / practical significance: The operational importance of an effect, separate from statistical evidence.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Original fictional case: Product manager Jules and experimentation analyst Priya review day one of a randomized test.
