TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

06Release Readiness and Go-to-MarketThe software is ready; the support handoff is not+

Your goal

Coordinate a release review by naming the missing operational prerequisite, its owner, and the distinction between review and launch approval.

This lesson in the book, page 49 ↗

A Read the situation

The software is ready; the support handoff is not

Release manager Ana and product marketer Rob prepare a Friday 10 a.m. launch review for a workspace-permissions update. Engineering checks are complete and the rollback rehearsal passed. The local launch checklist also requires migration notes delivered to Support and confirmed usable by its lead. Those notes have not been delivered. Ana can assign delivery for Thursday 3 p.m. and request Support review, but no waiver or launch approval exists. Rob wants to send the customer announcement now.

Ana: Release manager / Rob: Product marketer

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which readiness item is missing?

Answer and explanation

D. Migration notes delivered to Support and confirmed usable

The checklist requirement for Support's migration notes has not been met.

2. What does Friday 10 a.m. represent?

Answer and explanation

B. The planned launch review

The briefing identifies a review meeting, not a recorded authorization to launch.

3. What can Ana do now?

Answer and explanation

A. Assign note delivery and request Support review.

Ana can coordinate completion of the missing requirement but cannot invent its fulfillment or launch approval.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Engineering completion and a passed rollback rehearsal are positive readiness facts. They do not satisfy the separate Support requirement. The checklist calls for both delivery and confirmation that the notes are usable; a sent attachment alone would not necessarily close it.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
release readiness
The state of meeting requirements for a defined release decision.assess release readiness
go-to-market
Coordinated work to bring a product or change to its intended audience.align the go-to-market plan
launch checklist
The required items reviewed before a launch decision.complete the launch checklist
migration notes
Guidance explaining how users or teams move to a changed version or behavior.deliver migration notes
release notes
A summary of changes in a defined release.publish release notes
support enablement
Preparing support staff to assist users of the change.complete support enablement
known issue
A documented problem with a stated status and impact.disclose known issues
workaround
A defined alternative route around a known limitation.document the workaround
rollback
Returning to a prior state under a defined recovery procedure.rehearse rollback
rollout
Making a change available to a defined population.stage the rollout
feature flag
A control enabling or disabling a capability for defined conditions or users.configure a feature flag
go/no-go decision
A formal decision about whether a planned release proceeds.record the go/no-go decision
launch gate
A required condition before proceeding to a launch stage.satisfy the launch gate
operational readiness
Preparedness of the people and processes supporting live operation.verify operational readiness
runbook
Documented instructions for a defined operational process.update the runbook
escalation path
The route for obtaining higher-level help or authority.confirm the escalation path
customer announcement
Communication informing customers about a change or availability.approve the customer announcement
release owner
The role coordinating or accountable for the release under local rules.identify the release owner
dependency owner
The person accountable for a required input.name the dependency owner
readiness evidence
Records showing that a required release condition has been met.attach readiness evidence
acceptance confirmation
A recipient's recorded acknowledgment that an input meets the agreed need.obtain acceptance confirmation
contingency
A planned response if an expected condition is not met.agree the contingency
launch hold
A restriction preventing the launch from proceeding.communicate a launch hold
post-launch monitoring
Observation of defined product and operational signals after release.plan post-launch monitoring

Thursday 3 p.m. is a proposed delivery deadline for the missing input. Friday 10 a.m. is a review, not automatic approval. The customer announcement must not imply a launch decision before the responsible process has made one.

C Notice the language

Treat the handoff as part of readiness

Customer-facing teams need the information required to handle the change, not merely proof that engineering finished coding. Name what is missing and how it will be checked. A planned review can stay on the calendar while the launch decision remains conditional.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge completed work
Engineering checks are complete and rollback was rehearsed.
Name the open item
Support has not received the required migration notes.
Quote the actual gate
The notes must be delivered and confirmed usable.
Separate team readiness
Code readiness is not the whole launch checklist.
Assign the input
Name the owner for Thursday's note delivery.
Request recipient review
Ask the Support lead to confirm usability.
Avoid a false handoff
Sent does not automatically mean accepted.
Keep the review
Friday's go/no-go review can remain scheduled.
Qualify the launch
Launch approval is still pending.
Hold the announcement
Do not announce availability before the release decision.
Clarify the content
The notes need the changed permission behavior and support route.
Keep authority clear
No waiver has been approved.
Define the contingency
State what happens if the required confirmation is missing.
Preserve the evidence
Attach Support's confirmation to the readiness record.
Coordinate messages
Give Marketing and Support the same status.
Close the review request
Bring completed evidence and remaining blockers to the decision owner.
6 language and precision notes
Ready
Specify engineering, operational, commercial, or overall release readiness.
Delivered
A transmission event, not always evidence of recipient acceptance.
Usable
A recipient-facing condition, not a file-exists check.
Scheduled
A planned event, not a completed decision.
Announced
Creates customer expectations and should match actual approval.
Waived
Requires the appropriate authorized exception, not an informal assumption.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which readiness statement is accurate?

Answer and explanation

B. Engineering is ready; the required Support handoff remains incomplete.

The statement recognizes completed engineering work while preserving the separate open launch condition.

2. What evidence closes the stated Support item?

Answer and explanation

C. Delivery of the notes and the Support lead's usability confirmation

The checklist explicitly requires both delivery and confirmation that the recipient can use the notes.

3. Which announcement decision fits the facts?

Answer and explanation

C. Keep the customer availability announcement pending the release decision.

No launch approval or waiver exists, so the announcement must not represent either as settled.

4. What should happen if confirmation is still missing at the review?

Answer and explanation

D. Present the unresolved gate and follow the authorized go/no-go process.

An unmet requirement must remain visible to the decision process rather than be silently bypassed.

Check explanations in the book, page 95 ↗

E Conversations

The software is ready; the support handoff is not

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Ana: Release manager / Rob: Product marketer

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

  • go/no-go decision
  • acceptance confirmation

  • operational readiness

  • launch checklist

  2. Ana

    I will coordinate with the author and make sure Support can access the current document. A draft in a private folder would not meet the requirement.
  8. Ana
    Correct. The 4 need to reach the people using them. Their content should address the change and support route, not just repeat the marketing headline.

  10. Ana
    We need 5. Sent and accepted are different states here, and the readiness record should preserve both rather than assume silence means the document is sufficient.
  11. Rob
    We can keep Friday's ten o'clock meeting scheduled while those actions proceed. But the invitation should not be read as proof that the launch has already been approved.
  12. Ana
    Exactly. It is a 6 review. The decision depends on the required evidence and any open items, not on the fact that everyone has accepted the calendar invitation.

  14. Ana

  16. Ana

Answers and explanations
  1. launch checklist Turn 2. The launch checklist includes the Support migration-note requirement as well as engineering and rollback checks.
  2. Support enablement Turn 4. Support enablement prepares the team to assist customers with the change and is a required part of readiness here.
  3. dependency owner Turn 6. The dependency owner is responsible for producing the missing input, making the delivery request actionable.
  4. migration notes Turn 8. Migration notes explain how to move to or handle the changed behavior, and must actually reach Support in usable form.
  5. acceptance confirmation Turn 10. Acceptance confirmation records that Support considers the delivered notes usable, not merely that an attachment was transmitted.
  6. go/no-go decision Turn 12. The go/no-go decision is the pending authorization point, distinct from scheduling the meeting or completing engineering work.
  7. customer announcement Turn 14. The customer announcement must reflect actual availability and approval, so it should not get ahead of the pending release decision.
  8. contingency Turn 16. The contingency states the response if the required delivery or confirmation is missing, preserving the authorized decision process.
  9. readiness evidence Turn 18. Readiness evidence shows that the actual checklist condition has been met and can be reviewed by the decision owner.
  10. operational readiness Turn 20. Operational readiness includes the people and processes needed to support the live change, not only the software's technical state.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Rob: Engineering says the workspace-permissions update is ready, and the rollback rehearsal passed. I would like to send the customer announcement before Friday's review so the campaign can start.
  2. Ana: The launch checklist still has an open item. Support has not received the migration notes, and the local requirement also calls for its lead to confirm that they are usable.
  3. Rob: I had treated the notes as a follow-up document rather than part of the release decision. The checklist makes that dependency explicit, so we need to close it.
  4. Ana: Yes. Support enablement is part of this launch. The team needs to explain the changed permissions and route questions, not merely know that a new version exists.
  5. Rob: Can we set Thursday at three as the delivery deadline and ask the Support lead to review the material before the Friday meeting?
  6. Ana: We can assign a dependency owner and that deadline. We should also confirm who performs the recipient review so delivery does not become an unowned handoff.
  7. Rob: I will coordinate with the author and make sure Support can access the current document. A draft in a private folder would not meet the requirement.
  8. Ana: Correct. The migration notes need to reach the people using them. Their content should address the change and support route, not just repeat the marketing headline.
  9. Rob: I can show that we sent a file. Are you saying I also need the Support lead to confirm that it answers the migration questions?
  10. Ana: We need acceptance confirmation. Sent and accepted are different states here, and the readiness record should preserve both rather than assume silence means the document is sufficient.
  11. Rob: We can keep Friday's ten o'clock meeting scheduled while those actions proceed. But the invitation should not be read as proof that the launch has already been approved.
  12. Ana: Exactly. It is a go/no-go decision review. The decision depends on the required evidence and any open items, not on the fact that everyone has accepted the calendar invitation.
  13. Rob: I will prepare the announcement but keep it unsent. Please flag the release decision; I should not infer it from the meeting invitation.
  14. Ana: Keep the customer announcement aligned with the decision. We can prepare coordinated messages now without telling customers a pending change is already approved or available.
  15. Rob: If the notes arrive late or Support finds gaps, we need an explicit response. Otherwise people may assume the engineering result overrides the unfinished handoff.
  16. Ana: Agree the contingency with the release owner. The unresolved gate must be presented honestly; no waiver is recorded, and we should not invent one to preserve a preferred date.
  17. Rob: I will ask the Support lead to identify any missing explanation promptly. The author can then address it and bring the final confirmation to the review.
  18. Ana: Attach that readiness evidence to the release record. It should show what was delivered and accepted, not merely that an action was assigned to someone.
  19. Rob: Our status can now acknowledge the completed engineering checks while naming the remaining operational work. Marketing, Engineering, and Support will use the same wording.
  20. Ana: That gives an accurate operational readiness picture. We can keep preparing for the launch while leaving its approval with the responsible review and the evidence it requires.
Dialogue in the book, page 55 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Support has not received the migration notes

Fictional release handoff using the lesson case: a release is ready for review, but required migration notes have not reached support.

  1. Release manager: The release is ready for review. Have you received the migration notes required by our checklist?
  2. Support lead: No. We have the feature summary, but not the instructions explaining the customer migration changes.
  3. Release manager: Then support enablement remains incomplete. I shouldn't describe the overall launch handoff as finished.
  4. Support lead: Please identify the migration-note owner and the version we should review when it arrives.
  5. Release manager: I'll contact the owner and request the reviewed notes through our release channel.
  6. Support lead: Does receiving the file mean we have accepted the handoff, or should we confirm understanding too?
  7. Release manager: Please confirm you can access it and raise any unresolved support questions before readiness is assessed.
  8. Support lead: We also need the relevant rollback information identified, not assumed from an older release.
  9. Release manager: I'll include that request and keep launch readiness pending the required checklist review.
  10. Support lead: Understood. We'll acknowledge the actual materials received and make any remaining enablement gaps explicit.
A rollback link points to the previous release

Fictional release preparation: the current checklist links to a previous release's rollback notes; applicability has not been checked.

  1. Support engineer: The rollback link opens last month's notes. Are those instructions confirmed for this release?
  2. Release coordinator: Not yet. I'll ask the release owner to verify applicability rather than assume the link is sufficient.
  3. Support engineer: Please include the correct release identifier in the handoff so we can distinguish the versions.
  4. Release coordinator: Agreed. The current package is awaiting that check, even though the link itself opens successfully.
  5. Support engineer: Can I mark documentation accessible while keeping rollback readiness pending in our acceptance response?
  6. Release coordinator: Yes. Those are separate checks, and your acknowledgment should preserve that distinction.
  7. Support engineer: Who should receive questions if the verified notes describe a customer migration that support hasn't practiced?
  8. Release coordinator: I'll identify the migration owner and arrange a readiness discussion before the handoff is considered complete.
  9. Support engineer: I'll confirm receipt of the corrected package and repeat any constraints in our response.
  10. Release coordinator: Thank you. A working link won't be treated as evidence that the receiving team has applicable guidance.
Feature enabled is not customer migration complete

Fictional staged launch: a feature flag is enabled for a pilot group, while migration confirmation from that group is pending.

  1. Product manager: The pilot feature flag is enabled. Can support tell the pilot customers their migration is complete?
  2. Release engineer: Not yet. Enabling access and confirming each customer's migration are separate steps in this launch.
  3. Product manager: What should the receiving support team be able to confirm from your handoff today?
  4. Release engineer: The pilot scope, enabled configuration, and the fact that migration confirmation remains pending.
  5. Product manager: Please make clear that the feature isn't enabled for every customer in the wider release audience.
  6. Release engineer: I will. I'll include the pilot boundary and point to the migration-status owner.
  7. Product manager: Should support use the old rollout announcement as their customer response until a new one arrives?
  8. Release engineer: Only reviewed, applicable communication should be used; the old announcement may describe a different scope.
  9. Product manager: I'll coordinate the current wording and ask support to acknowledge the pilot limitation explicitly.
  10. Release engineer: Then our handoff will distinguish enabled access, confirmed migration, and broader launch readiness accurately.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

The software is ready; the support handoff is not
  1. Read turns 1-10. Contrast sent with confirmed usable.
  2. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Stress review, approval, and announcement as separate events.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

A sent file is only half the gate

The checklist requires a migration guide delivered to Support and confirmed usable by its lead. Delivery is recorded, but confirmation is absent. The launch review is tomorrow; no approval is recorded.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Release: "The guide reached Support, so we can record ___."

Answer and explanation

C. delivery

Delivery is the recorded transfer of the guide, not acceptance of its contents.

2. Support: "You still need my usability ___ before closing that item."

Answer and explanation

A. confirmation

The recipient must confirm usability; sending the document does not establish this.

3. Release: "We will take the open item to the launch ___ tomorrow."

Answer and explanation

B. review

Tomorrow is the scheduled review, not a recorded decision or completed handoff.

4. Marketing: "I will keep the availability announcement unsent until we have launch ___."

Answer and explanation

C. approval

Approval is still absent, so Marketing cannot announce an authorized launch yet.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Original fictional case: Release manager Ana and product marketer Rob prepare a Friday 10 a.m. launch review for a workspace-permissions update. Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

07Platform, APIs, and Technical DebtA three-day adapter or a ten-day interface+

Your goal

Explain a short-term delivery option and its maintenance consequences without hiding compatibility, ownership, or retirement requirements.

This lesson in the book, page 58 ↗

A Read the situation

A three-day adapter or a ten-day interface

Product manager Camille and engineering lead Dev assess a customer request due in five working days. A client-specific adapter is estimated at three days including tests and could support a single-client pilot. A shared API change is estimated at ten days including tests. The adapter duplicates an existing transformation and needs named ownership, monitoring, rollback, and an approved 30-day expiry or extension process. Existing clients must retain their current behavior. Neither option is approved, and no engineering capacity beyond the estimates is confirmed.

Camille: Product manager / Dev: Engineering lead

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which estimate fits inside the requested five-day window?

Answer and explanation

C. The three-day adapter estimate

Three days is within five, while ten exceeds it; feasibility and authorization still require the stated conditions.

2. What does the adapter duplicate?

Answer and explanation

B. An existing transformation

The supplied concern is duplicated transformation logic, creating a maintenance responsibility.

3. Which constraint applies to existing clients?

Answer and explanation

D. Their current behavior must be preserved.

The briefing explicitly requires existing clients to retain their current behavior.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The three-day estimate fits the five-day request numerically; it is not yet an approved commitment. Its scope is a single-client pilot. The ten-day shared API option exceeds the requested window, so the team must change scope, timing, or the decision rather than hide the difference.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
platform capability
A shared function supporting multiple products or consumers.invest in platform capability
API
Application programming interface, a defined way for software components to interact.define the API contract
API contract
The agreed inputs, outputs, and behavior of an interface.preserve the API contract
endpoint
An addressable operation or resource in an interface.document the endpoint
request schema
The defined structure and meaning of incoming data.validate the request schema
response schema
The defined structure and meaning of returned data.preserve the response schema
backward compatibility
Continued support for existing consumers under the stated contract.maintain backward compatibility
breaking change
A change that can invalidate existing consumers' expectations.identify a breaking change
versioning
Identifying and managing distinct interface or product versions.plan API versioning
deprecation
Notice that a capability is discouraged and may be retired under a defined plan.communicate deprecation
migration path
The supported route from an old interface or behavior to a new one.provide a migration path
adapter
A component translating between interfaces or data forms.scope the adapter
client-specific logic
Behavior implemented for a particular consumer.isolate client-specific logic
duplicated logic
The same rule or transformation maintained in multiple places.track duplicated logic
technical debt
Additional future work caused by expedient or inadequate design or implementation.make technical debt explicit
maintenance burden
The effort and risk involved in keeping a solution working.estimate the maintenance burden
ownership
Clear responsibility for a component or decision.assign ownership
observability
The ability to understand a system through its exposed signals.provide observability
rollback plan
The defined way to reverse a change if necessary.test the rollback plan
expiry condition
A rule stating when a temporary option ends unless explicitly extended.set the expiry condition
retirement plan
The agreed work and timing for removing an old or temporary path.fund the retirement plan
extension decision
A recorded decision to continue beyond an original limit.require an extension decision
blast radius
The scope of users or systems affected by a change or failure.limit the blast radius
service-level objective
A defined target for a service measure, abbreviated SLO.agree a service-level objective

A temporary adapter remains a maintained product path until removed. The 30-day limit needs a named owner and an approved expiry or extension process. Interface compatibility includes behavior and expectations, not just keeping an endpoint name unchanged.

C Notice the language

Translate debt into an owned consequence

Technical debt is not a synonym for bad code or an automatic reason to reject a short-term option. Describe what is duplicated, what must be maintained, and how the temporary path ends. A product decision can then weigh timing and consequences without pretending the shortcut is free.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Set out the options
The adapter is estimated at three days; the shared API work at ten.
Qualify the estimate
Both estimates include tests but are not delivery approvals.
Name the short-term scope
The adapter would serve one client's pilot.
Expose the duplication
It repeats an existing transformation.
Translate the debt
Both copies would need to stay consistent until retirement.
Protect existing clients
Their current behavior must remain supported.
Ask about the contract
Would this change alter an existing response or behavior?
Limit exposure
Keep the pilot's blast radius explicit.
Assign maintenance
Who owns the adapter after the first delivery?
Require monitoring
Which signals show that the temporary path is working?
Plan reversal
The pilot needs a tested rollback plan.
Set the limit
The proposed adapter expires after thirty days unless explicitly extended.
Avoid silent permanence
A missed retirement date requires a decision, not an assumption.
Compare honestly
The durable option needs a longer delivery window.
Keep approval visible
Neither approach is approved yet.
Close the proposal
Present timing, scope, compatibility, ownership, and retirement together.
6 language and precision notes
Quick
Specify what is included and what future work remains.
Temporary
Needs an expiry or retirement decision to have practical meaning.
Debt
Describe its consequence instead of using it as a moral label.
Compatible
Includes behavior, not only unchanged field names.
Shared
A wider consumer base can increase both value and change impact.
Estimate
A planning input, not automatically a staffed commitment.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which proposal accurately describes the adapter?

Answer and explanation

C. A three-day estimated single-client pilot with required ownership, monitoring, rollback, and expiry controls

The adapter's timing advantage comes with a narrow scope and explicit unresolved operating and retirement requirements.

2. Why does duplicated transformation logic matter?

Answer and explanation

D. Future changes may require both copies to stay consistent.

Maintaining the same transformation in two places creates a concrete consistency and ownership burden.

3. What is a compatibility question?

Answer and explanation

C. Will existing clients receive behavior they can still handle under the current contract?

Compatibility concerns consumers' actual expectations and supported behavior, not labels or assumed adoption.

4. What should happen at the 30-day limit?

Answer and explanation

B. Follow the approved expiry or explicit extension process.

The time limit requires a controlled decision or retirement, not silent continuation or an invented replacement.

E Conversations

A three-day adapter or a ten-day interface

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Camille: Product manager / Dev: Engineering lead

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • blast radius
  • API contract
  • expiry condition
  • retirement plan
  • duplicated logic
  • ownership
  • Maintenance burden
  • breaking change
  • rollback plan
  • adapter
  1. Camille
    The customer needs a response within five working days. We have a three-day adapter estimate and ten days for shared API work, both including tests. What can we responsibly offer?
  2. Dev
    The 1 could fit the requested window as a single-client pilot. That is an option to assess, not an approved delivery commitment or a solution for every consumer.
  3. Camille
    I want to explain why the quicker path has a cost beyond those three days. The estimate says it repeats a transformation we already maintain elsewhere.
  4. Dev
    That creates 2. If the rule changes, both paths need to stay consistent until the adapter is removed. The customer-specific route cannot become invisible once the pilot starts.
  5. Camille
    Then technical debt should be described as that future consistency work and the responsibility to retire the temporary path, not simply as engineers disliking a quick solution.
  6. Dev
    Exactly. 3 makes the consequence concrete. We can choose a bounded shortcut deliberately, but we should not present it as having no ongoing owner or cost.
  7. Camille
    The shared option also needs to preserve existing clients. We cannot change what they receive merely because the new customer has a different need.
  8. Dev
    Review the 4. Existing behavior must remain supported. Keeping the same endpoint name would not be enough if we changed the meaning of a field or response.
  9. Camille
    We should distinguish a new optional capability from a change that older clients cannot handle. That will help me explain the engineering review in product terms.
  10. Dev
    A 5 can be behavioral, not just structural. We need to assess the actual consumers and contract rather than promise compatibility from a superficial comparison.
  11. Camille
    One client sounds contained, but can another client hit that path accidentally? I need to understand the boundary and how we will detect a problem.
  12. Dev
    Define the 6 and the relevant monitoring. Narrow scope is useful only if the implementation and controls actually preserve that boundary.
  13. Camille
    Who will support it after delivery? I do not want the team to assume the first implementer will maintain it indefinitely without agreed capacity.
  14. Dev
    Assign 7 before approval. The proposal must identify who watches the signals, handles issues, and keeps the temporary transformation consistent with the existing one.
  15. Camille
    If we switch it off, what happens to that customer? Can we test the return path before offering the pilot, rather than work it out during an incident?
  16. Dev
    Include a tested 8. The decision needs a practical recovery route, not just confidence that the change is small.
  17. Camille
    We proposed a thirty-day limit. At that point the adapter should expire or receive an explicit extension under an approved process, not remain by accident.
  18. Dev
    Make that 9 part of the proposal. Temporary is only useful language if the end condition has an owner and a decision route.
  19. Camille
    I will present the options with the timing difference, the single-client limit, compatibility requirements, and ongoing work. Neither will be described as approved until the relevant review is complete.
  20. Dev
    Include the 10 alongside the estimates. Then the team can decide whether the bounded pilot is worthwhile or whether the customer timeline should move for the shared solution.

Answers and explanations
  1. adapter Turn 2. The adapter is the narrow translation component estimated at three days, potentially serving a single-client pilot rather than the shared product.
  2. duplicated logic Turn 4. Duplicated logic means maintaining the same transformation in more than one place, creating future consistency work.
  3. Maintenance burden Turn 6. Maintenance burden is the ongoing effort and risk of keeping the temporary path correct, monitored, and consistent.
  4. API contract Turn 8. The API contract includes the supported inputs, outputs, and behavior on which existing consumers depend.
  5. breaking change Turn 10. A breaking change can invalidate existing client expectations even when the visible endpoint or field names remain similar.
  6. blast radius Turn 12. Blast radius is the scope of affected consumers and systems, which the single-client pilot must explicitly bound.
  7. ownership Turn 14. Ownership names responsibility for operating and maintaining the adapter after its initial implementation.
  8. rollback plan Turn 16. The rollback plan defines how the pilot can be reversed if necessary, rather than assuming temporary code is automatically easy to remove.
  9. expiry condition Turn 18. The expiry condition makes the thirty-day limit actionable through retirement or an explicit authorized extension.
  10. retirement plan Turn 20. The retirement plan specifies how the temporary path will be removed or replaced, so its future work is visible in the choice.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Camille: The customer needs a response within five working days. We have a three-day adapter estimate and ten days for shared API work, both including tests. What can we responsibly offer?
  2. Dev: The adapter could fit the requested window as a single-client pilot. That is an option to assess, not an approved delivery commitment or a solution for every consumer.

  5. Camille: Then technical debt should be described as that future consistency work and the responsibility to retire the temporary path, not simply as engineers disliking a quick solution.
  6. Dev: Exactly. Maintenance burden makes the consequence concrete. We can choose a bounded shortcut deliberately, but we should not present it as having no ongoing owner or cost.
  7. Camille: The shared option also needs to preserve existing clients. We cannot change what they receive merely because the new customer has a different need.
  8. Dev: Review the API contract. Existing behavior must remain supported. Keeping the same endpoint name would not be enough if we changed the meaning of a field or response.
  9. Camille: We should distinguish a new optional capability from a change that older clients cannot handle. That will help me explain the engineering review in product terms.
  10. Dev: A breaking change can be behavioral, not just structural. We need to assess the actual consumers and contract rather than promise compatibility from a superficial comparison.
  11. Camille: One client sounds contained, but can another client hit that path accidentally? I need to understand the boundary and how we will detect a problem.
  12. Dev: Define the blast radius and the relevant monitoring. Narrow scope is useful only if the implementation and controls actually preserve that boundary.

  14. Dev: Assign ownership before approval. The proposal must identify who watches the signals, handles issues, and keeps the temporary transformation consistent with the existing one.

Dialogue in the book, page 64 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Fast workaround or a stronger interface

Fictional platform discussion using the lesson case: a workaround could deliver sooner, while improving the underlying interface would take longer.

  1. Business sponsor: Can we choose the workaround since it delivers the requested feature sooner?
  2. Product manager: We can compare it with the interface improvement, but speed shouldn't be our only criterion.
  3. Business sponsor: What else belongs beside delivery time when explaining this tradeoff to nontechnical stakeholders?
  4. Product manager: Maintenance effort, reliability evidence, affected users, and the cost of revisiting the workaround later.
  5. Business sponsor: Is that what you mean by technical debt: work we may need to repay through future changes?
  6. Product manager: Yes, broadly. Here we should describe the specific maintenance obligation rather than use the phrase as a warning.
  7. Business sponsor: Do we have confirmed reliability results for both options, or are we comparing assumptions?
  8. Product manager: Those results are not provided yet. We'll label estimates and request the engineering assessment.
  9. Business sponsor: I'll consider a bounded workaround if its limits and follow-up ownership are explicit.
  10. Product manager: I'll bring comparable options and conditions before asking you to choose a delivery approach.
One customer exception or a reusable API change

Fictional platform planning: one customer requests an extra response field; the team can assess a customer-specific exception or a shared API change.

  1. Account manager: Could engineering add the field only for this customer so we avoid a platform discussion?
  2. Platform product manager: That option needs comparison with a shared application programming interface, or API, change.
  3. Account manager: Why consider a broader change when only one customer has asked for the field so far?
  4. Platform product manager: A special case may add maintenance and testing obligations. A shared change may affect more consumers.
  5. Account manager: So neither option is automatically better simply because its visible scope looks smaller or larger.
  6. Platform product manager: Correct. Compare delivery effort, compatibility, reliability checks, and future support using the same criteria.
  7. Account manager: Can you promise the exception now while the shared design is still being assessed?
  8. Platform product manager: No. We haven't established its effort or safety for existing consumers, so timing remains unconfirmed.
  9. Account manager: I'll tell the customer the request is under assessment and explain which tradeoff we are reviewing.
  10. Platform product manager: I'll return with bounded options rather than make a platform commitment from an unreviewed exception.
A manual retry hides recurring maintenance work

Fictional product operations: a failing import sometimes succeeds after a manual retry; the cause and frequency are not yet quantified.

  1. Operations lead: Manual retry often completes the import. Could we keep that workaround instead of prioritizing a fix?
  2. Product manager: We should compare its ongoing support burden with the effort and expected benefit of investigating the interface.
  3. Operations lead: We don't have a reliable failure frequency yet, so the burden is still an estimate.
  4. Product manager: Then let's measure incidents and staff time before calling the workaround cheap or the fix unnecessary.
  5. Operations lead: Would you label this technical debt even though the immediate customer task can eventually finish?
  6. Product manager: The recurring manual obligation is the useful detail. The label alone doesn't establish priority or cause.
  7. Operations lead: Can we keep reporting successful retries while making the unresolved reliability issue visible?
  8. Product manager: Yes. Recovery success and first-attempt reliability answer different questions and should be tracked separately.
  9. Operations lead: I'll bring a consistent record of failures, retries, and handling time to the next prioritization review.
  10. Product manager: Then we can compare options on evidence instead of assuming hidden work costs nothing.

The broader interface work is estimated at eight days.

Answer and explanation

Answer and explanation

The briefing states that neither proposal has been approved.

Lesson 07: Platform, APIs, and Technical Debt
Original fictional case: Product manager Camille and engineering lead Dev assess a customer request due in five working days. A client-specific adapter is estimated at three days including tests and could support a single-client pilot. A shared API change is estimated at ten days including tests. The adapter duplicates an existing transformation and needs named ownership, monitoring, rollback, and an approved 30-day expiry or extension process. Existing clients must retain their current behavior. Neither option is approved, and no engineering capacity beyond the estimates is confirmed.
Language workshop: A three-day adapter or a ten-day interface
Communication goal: Explain a short-term delivery option and its maintenance consequences without hiding compatibility, ownership, or retirement requirements.
Language guidance: The three-day estimate fits the five-day request numerically; it is not yet an approved commitment. Its scope is a single-client pilot. The ten-day shared API option exceeds the requested window, so the team must change scope, timing, or the decision rather than hide the difference.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The adapter is estimated at three days; the shared API work at ten. / Both estimates include tests but are not delivery approvals. / The adapter would serve one client's pilot. / It repeats an existing transformation. / Both copies would need to stay consistent until retirement. / Their current behavior must remain supported. / Would this change alter an existing response or behavior? / Keep the pilot's blast radius explicit. / Who owns the adapter after the first delivery? / Which signals show that the temporary path is working? / The pilot needs a tested rollback plan. / The proposed adapter expires after thirty days unless explicitly extended. / A missed retirement date requires a decision, not an assumption. / The durable option needs a longer delivery window. / Neither approach is approved yet. / Present timing, scope, compatibility, ownership, and retirement together.
Vocabulary: platform capability: A shared function supporting multiple products or consumers. / API: Application programming interface, a defined way for software components to interact. / API contract: The agreed inputs, outputs, and behavior of an interface. / endpoint: An addressable operation or resource in an interface. / request schema: The defined structure and meaning of incoming data. / response schema: The defined structure and meaning of returned data. / backward compatibility: Continued support for existing consumers under the stated contract. / breaking change: A change that can invalidate existing consumers' expectations. / versioning: Identifying and managing distinct interface or product versions. / deprecation: Notice that a capability is discouraged and may be retired under a defined plan. / migration path: The supported route from an old interface or behavior to a new one. / adapter: A component translating between interfaces or data forms. / client-specific logic: Behavior implemented for a particular consumer. / duplicated logic: The same rule or transformation maintained in multiple places. / technical debt: Additional future work caused by expedient or inadequate design or implementation. / maintenance burden: The effort and risk involved in keeping a solution working. / ownership: Clear responsibility for a component or decision. / observability: The ability to understand a system through its exposed signals. / rollback plan: The defined way to reverse a change if necessary. / expiry condition: A rule stating when a temporary option ends unless explicitly extended. / retirement plan: The agreed work and timing for removing an old or temporary path. / extension decision: A recorded decision to continue beyond an original limit. / blast radius: The scope of users or systems affected by a change or failure. / service-level objective: A defined target for a service measure, abbreviated SLO.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10. Stress estimated timing and the compatibility requirement. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Pause before ownership, rollback, and expiry conditions. Check the transfer. Read two, eight, and twenty-one with the correct time basis.
Partner: Camille: Product manager / Dev: Engineering lead
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A single-client adapter is estimated at two days; shared interface work needs eight. The requested window is four days. The adapter requires an owner and expires after 21 days unless explicitly extended. Neither option is approved.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Camille: We proposed a thirty-day limit. At that point the adapter should expire or receive an explicit extension under an approved process, not remain by accident.
Dev: Make that expiry condition part of the proposal. Temporary is only useful language if the end condition has an owner and a decision route.
Camille: I will present the options with the timing difference, the single-client limit, compatibility requirements, and ongoing work. Neither will be described as approved until the relevant review is complete.
Dev: Include the retirement plan alongside the estimates. Then the team can decide whether the bounded pilot is worthwhile or whether the customer timeline should move for the shared solution.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

REFERENCE
Course: Software Product Management English
Professional audience: software product managers, product owners, UX leads, engineering managers, growth product teams, platform PMs, and product-operations staff
Scope: Original fictional English practice, not a product-management certification, statistical analysis service, software security assessment, or release authorization. All teams, data, dates, and decisions are invented. Actual work must follow the organization's responsibilities, research safeguards, engineering controls, experiment methods, and applicable requirements.
Lesson 07: Platform, APIs, and Technical Debt
Original fictional case: Product manager Camille and engineering lead Dev assess a customer request due in five working days. A client-specific adapter is estimated at three days including tests and could support a single-client pilot. A shared API change is estimated at ten days including tests. The adapter duplicates an existing transformation and needs named ownership, monitoring, rollback, and an approved 30-day expiry or extension process. Existing clients must retain their current behavior. Neither option is approved, and no engineering capacity beyond the estimates is confirmed.
Language workshop: A three-day adapter or a ten-day interface
Communication goal: Explain a short-term delivery option and its maintenance consequences without hiding compatibility, ownership, or retirement requirements.
Language guidance: The three-day estimate fits the five-day request numerically; it is not yet an approved commitment. Its scope is a single-client pilot. The ten-day shared API option exceeds the requested window, so the team must change scope, timing, or the decision rather than hide the difference.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The adapter is estimated at three days; the shared API work at ten. / Both estimates include tests but are not delivery approvals. / The adapter would serve one client's pilot. / It repeats an existing transformation. / Both copies would need to stay consistent until retirement. / Their current behavior must remain supported. / Would this change alter an existing response or behavior? / Keep the pilot's blast radius explicit. / Who owns the adapter after the first delivery? / Which signals show that the temporary path is working? / The pilot needs a tested rollback plan. / The proposed adapter expires after thirty days unless explicitly extended. / A missed retirement date requires a decision, not an assumption. / The durable option needs a longer delivery window. / Neither approach is approved yet. / Present timing, scope, compatibility, ownership, and retirement together.
Vocabulary: platform capability: A shared function supporting multiple products or consumers. / API: Application programming interface, a defined way for software components to interact. / API contract: The agreed inputs, outputs, and behavior of an interface. / endpoint: An addressable operation or resource in an interface. / request schema: The defined structure and meaning of incoming data. / response schema: The defined structure and meaning of returned data. / backward compatibility: Continued support for existing consumers under the stated contract. / breaking change: A change that can invalidate existing consumers' expectations. / versioning: Identifying and managing distinct interface or product versions. / deprecation: Notice that a capability is discouraged and may be retired under a defined plan. / migration path: The supported route from an old interface or behavior to a new one. / adapter: A component translating between interfaces or data forms. / client-specific logic: Behavior implemented for a particular consumer. / duplicated logic: The same rule or transformation maintained in multiple places. / technical debt: Additional future work caused by expedient or inadequate design or implementation. / maintenance burden: The effort and risk involved in keeping a solution working. / ownership: Clear responsibility for a component or decision. / observability: The ability to understand a system through its exposed signals. / rollback plan: The defined way to reverse a change if necessary. / expiry condition: A rule stating when a temporary option ends unless explicitly extended. / retirement plan: The agreed work and timing for removing an old or temporary path. / extension decision: A recorded decision to continue beyond an original limit. / blast radius: The scope of users or systems affected by a change or failure. / service-level objective: A defined target for a service measure, abbreviated SLO.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10. Stress estimated timing and the compatibility requirement. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Pause before ownership, rollback, and expiry conditions. Check the transfer. Read two, eight, and twenty-one with the correct time basis.
Partner: Camille: Product manager / Dev: Engineering lead
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A single-client adapter is estimated at two days; shared interface work needs eight. The requested window is four days. The adapter requires an owner and expires after 21 days unless explicitly extended. Neither option is approved.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Camille: We proposed a thirty-day limit. At that point the adapter should expire or receive an explicit extension under an approved process, not remain by accident.
Dev: Make that expiry condition part of the proposal. Temporary is only useful language if the end condition has an owner and a decision route.
Camille: I will present the options with the timing difference, the single-client limit, compatibility requirements, and ongoing work. Neither will be described as approved until the relevant review is complete.
Dev: Include the retirement plan alongside the estimates. Then the team can decide whether the bounded pilot is worthwhile or whether the customer timeline should move for the shared solution.
END REFERENCE
08Stakeholder Pushback and Executive NarrativeTurn competing opinions into a decision request+

Your goal

Present a concise recommendation tied to a shared objective while acknowledging evidence limits, displaced work, and decision authority.

This lesson in the book, page 67 ↗

A Read the situation

Turn competing opinions into a decision request

Product lead Iris prepares a decision with executive chair Malik. The agreed quarterly objective is to reduce support friction for existing paid teams. Option A is a two-week reliability pilot addressing an error category present in 40 of 100 reviewed support tickets. Those tickets are not 40 distinct customers, and no retention effect is established. Option B is a sales dashboard requested for uncommitted prospects. Capacity allows only one pilot. The agreed criterion favors documented existing-customer friction; the chair makes the decision. Iris recommends A, with B deferred and results reviewed after two weeks.

Iris: Product lead / Malik: Executive decision chair

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is the shared quarterly objective?

Answer and explanation

A. Reduce support friction for existing paid teams

The briefing identifies support friction among existing paid teams as the agreed objective.

2. What does 40 of 100 mean?

Answer and explanation

D. Forty reviewed tickets belong to the relevant error category.

The count concerns reviewed tickets, not distinct customers, population prevalence, or retention effects.

3. Who makes the supplied decision?

Answer and explanation

C. Malik, the executive chair

The case explicitly assigns decision authority to the executive chair.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Forty of 100 reviewed tickets is 40% of those tickets. It is not 40 distinct customers or 40% of the customer population. The case supplies no causal retention effect. A recommendation can be well supported by problem fit while its future impact remains uncertain.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
executive narrative
A concise explanation connecting evidence, trade-offs, and a decision.shape the executive narrative
decision request
A specific choice or authorization sought from a decision maker.state the decision request
shared objective
An agreed outcome used to align competing views.confirm the shared objective
decision criterion
A defined basis for evaluating alternatives.agree the decision criterion
recommendation
A proposed course of action supported by reasons.lead with the recommendation
evidence strength
The degree of support the available information gives a claim.qualify evidence strength
customer friction
Difficulty users encounter while trying to achieve a task.reduce customer friction
paid team
A customer organization or group with a paid product relationship.support existing paid teams
ticket category
A defined grouping of support records by issue type.analyze the ticket category
distinct customer
A unique customer under the stated identity rule.count distinct customers
retention effect
A change in continued use attributable to a specified intervention.evaluate the retention effect
prospect
A potential customer who has not yet made the relevant commitment.distinguish prospects from customers
uncommitted pipeline
Potential business without the stated binding or accepted commitment.qualify uncommitted pipeline
pilot scope
The bounded population, work, and duration of a trial.define the pilot scope
decision owner
The role authorized to make a specified choice.identify the decision owner
displaced work
Work that does not proceed because another option uses capacity.name the displaced work
counterargument
A reason offered against a proposed conclusion or action.address the counterargument
steelman
Presenting the strongest reasonable version of another position.steelman the alternative
assumption check
Review of a premise on which a proposal depends.conduct an assumption check
reversibility
The extent to which a decision can be undone at acceptable cost.assess reversibility
decision boundary
The scope of what a particular approval does and does not authorize.state the decision boundary
success measure
A defined indicator used to evaluate the intended result.agree a success measure
follow-up review
A scheduled evaluation of progress or results after a decision.schedule the follow-up review
decision record
Documentation of the choice, basis, authority, and conditions.preserve the decision record

The agreed criterion favors A over B for this one available pilot. That does not prove B has no value or authorize a full rollout. The chair must decide the bounded pilot; the two-week review evaluates results rather than guaranteeing success in advance.

C Notice the language

Recommend clearly without overstating certainty

Executives need a decision-ready explanation, not a history of every meeting. Lead with the recommendation, connect it to the shared objective and evidence, state the trade-off, and identify the decision requested. Acknowledge the alternative fairly rather than caricaturing the executive who prefers it.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Lead with the action
I recommend the two-week reliability pilot.
Connect the objective
It directly addresses documented friction for existing paid teams.
State the evidence
The error category appears in 40 of 100 reviewed tickets.
Protect the unit
Those are tickets, not 40 distinct customers.
Limit the outcome claim
We have not established a retention effect.
Represent the alternative
The dashboard could support prospect conversations, but the pipeline is uncommitted.
Apply the shared rule
Our criterion favors documented existing-customer friction.
State the trade-off
Choosing A defers the sales-dashboard pilot.
Name the authority
I am asking the chair to approve this bounded pilot.
Define the boundary
This is not approval for a full rollout.
Invite a relevant challenge
Which assumption would change your view under the agreed objective?
Avoid personal framing
The disagreement concerns priorities, not the competence of either team.
Set the review
Review the pilot evidence after two weeks.
Keep the measure honest
Agree the success measure before interpreting results.
Record the decision
Capture the chosen scope, rationale, and owner.
Close the request
Approve or decline the pilot on the stated basis.
6 language and precision notes
Recommend
A clear proposal, not an announcement that approval already occurred.
Evidence
State its unit, source, and limits.
Customer
Do not substitute ticket counts for unique organizations.
Pipeline
Potential business is not automatically committed revenue.
Pilot
A bounded trial, not a hidden full rollout.
Review
A point to assess results, not a promise that results will be positive.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which opening is most decision-ready?

Answer and explanation

A. I recommend the two-week reliability pilot because it matches our agreed objective and documented ticket evidence.

The opening states the action, duration, objective fit, and evidence without personal attack or unsupported outcomes.

2. How should Iris describe the alternative?

Answer and explanation

A. It could support prospect conversations, but the supplied pipeline is uncommitted.

A fair comparison recognizes possible value while preserving the actual commitment status.

3. What does approval of the requested pilot authorize?

Answer and explanation

B. The defined two-week pilot within its agreed scope

The request is explicitly bounded to one pilot and does not authorize unrelated work or establish outcomes.

4. Which statement about the ticket evidence is accurate?

Answer and explanation

B. It identifies a documented issue category but does not establish distinct-customer count or retention impact.

The supplied unit is reviewed tickets, and the broader customer and causal claims are expressly unestablished.

E Conversations

Turn competing opinions into a decision request

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Iris: Product lead / Malik: Executive decision chair

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • shared objective
  • decision boundary
  • uncommitted pipeline
  • retention effect
  • displaced work
  • follow-up review
  • ticket category
  • success measure
  • recommendation
  • decision record
  1. Malik
    We keep debating reliability for customers and a dashboard for prospects. What decision are you asking us to make?
  2. Iris
    My 1 is to approve the two-week reliability pilot. It addresses the documented error category and fits the objective we agreed for existing paid teams.
  3. Malik
    State that objective explicitly. The executives have been using different ideas of success, which makes each option sound essential on its own terms.
  4. Iris
    The 2 is to reduce support friction for current paid teams this quarter. Under that objective, the ticket evidence gives A a more direct basis than uncommitted prospect requests.
  5. Malik
    What exactly does the evidence show? I heard someone say forty customers are affected, but the briefing refers to forty tickets out of one hundred reviewed.
  6. Iris
    It is a 3 count, not forty distinct customers. The category appears in forty of the hundred reviewed tickets, and we should preserve that unit when presenting the problem.
  7. Malik
    Does that also mean the reliability work will improve retention? That would be useful, but I do not want the recommendation to promise a result we have not measured.
  8. Iris
    No 4 is established. We are proposing to address documented friction, not claiming the ticket count proves a future reduction in customer loss.
  9. Malik
    Sales will challenge that. What is the strongest case for their dashboard? Tell me what we give up, not just what supports your recommendation.
  10. Iris
    It could support prospect conversations, but the supplied 5 is not guaranteed business. That potential matters, yet it is less directly supported under the criterion chosen for this quarter.
  11. Malik
    Capacity permits one pilot. If I approve A, the dashboard pilot does not happen at the same time, and that consequence should be visible to the commercial team.
  12. Iris
    I will name the 6. B is deferred, not declared worthless. Any later reconsideration should use updated evidence and the available capacity rather than an implied promise that it is already next.
  13. Malik
    How broad is the approval you want? I do not want a small pilot decision interpreted as permission to roll out a change across the entire customer base.
  14. Iris
    The 7 is the defined two-week pilot. We should record its scope and responsible owner, with any wider rollout requiring its own evidence and decision.
  15. Malik
    We also need to agree what we will examine afterward. Otherwise each team may select whichever number makes its original preference look right.
  16. Iris
    Agree the 8 before interpreting the results. It should connect to the friction we are addressing, with the limits of the pilot evidence kept visible.
  17. Malik
    Put the result review in for two weeks. Bring the same measures whether the pilot succeeds or disappoints; we need an actual decision at that meeting.
  18. Iris
    The 9 will show what changed, what remains uncertain, and what decision follows. The review date does not promise a retention result or automatic expansion.
  19. Malik
    The request is clear: one bounded pilot, an explicit trade-off, and a defined review. I will decide on that basis and record any conditions.
  20. Iris
    I will preserve the 10 with your choice and conditions. That gives both teams the same account of the decision, including what was approved and what was deferred.

Answers and explanations
  1. recommendation Turn 2. The recommendation is a specific proposed action, the two-week reliability pilot, rather than a claim that approval already exists.
  2. shared objective Turn 4. The shared objective is reducing support friction for existing paid teams, providing the common basis for this decision.
  3. ticket category Turn 6. A ticket category groups support records; forty tickets in it do not establish forty distinct customers.
  4. retention effect Turn 8. A retention effect would require evidence about continued use and attribution, neither of which is established by the ticket count.
  5. uncommitted pipeline Turn 10. Uncommitted pipeline represents potential business without the relevant confirmed commitment, limiting the strength of the dashboard's current case.
  6. displaced work Turn 12. Displaced work is the sales-dashboard pilot that cannot proceed alongside A under the one-pilot capacity limit.
  7. decision boundary Turn 14. The decision boundary limits the requested authorization to the defined two-week pilot, not an unrestricted rollout.
  8. success measure Turn 16. The success measure defines how the pilot will be evaluated before results are interpreted, avoiding a retrospective change of criteria.
  9. follow-up review Turn 18. The follow-up review evaluates the pilot evidence after two weeks without guaranteeing a positive result in advance.
  10. decision record Turn 20. The decision record captures the authorized choice, scope, rationale, and conditions so teams do not infer more than was decided.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Malik: We keep debating reliability for customers and a dashboard for prospects. What decision are you asking us to make?
  2. Iris: My recommendation is to approve the two-week reliability pilot. It addresses the documented error category and fits the objective we agreed for existing paid teams.
  3. Malik: State that objective explicitly. The executives have been using different ideas of success, which makes each option sound essential on its own terms.
  4. Iris: The shared objective is to reduce support friction for current paid teams this quarter. Under that objective, the ticket evidence gives A a more direct basis than uncommitted prospect requests.
  5. Malik: What exactly does the evidence show? I heard someone say forty customers are affected, but the briefing refers to forty tickets out of one hundred reviewed.
  6. Iris: It is a ticket category count, not forty distinct customers. The category appears in forty of the hundred reviewed tickets, and we should preserve that unit when presenting the problem.
  7. Malik: Does that also mean the reliability work will improve retention? That would be useful, but I do not want the recommendation to promise a result we have not measured.
  8. Iris: No retention effect is established. We are proposing to address documented friction, not claiming the ticket count proves a future reduction in customer loss.
  9. Malik: Sales will challenge that. What is the strongest case for their dashboard? Tell me what we give up, not just what supports your recommendation.
  10. Iris: It could support prospect conversations, but the supplied uncommitted pipeline is not guaranteed business. That potential matters, yet it is less directly supported under the criterion chosen for this quarter.
  11. Malik: Capacity permits one pilot. If I approve A, the dashboard pilot does not happen at the same time, and that consequence should be visible to the commercial team.
  12. Iris: I will name the displaced work. B is deferred, not declared worthless. Any later reconsideration should use updated evidence and the available capacity rather than an implied promise that it is already next.
  13. Malik: How broad is the approval you want? I do not want a small pilot decision interpreted as permission to roll out a change across the entire customer base.
  14. Iris: The decision boundary is the defined two-week pilot. We should record its scope and responsible owner, with any wider rollout requiring its own evidence and decision.
  15. Malik: We also need to agree what we will examine afterward. Otherwise each team may select whichever number makes its original preference look right.
  16. Iris: Agree the success measure before interpreting the results. It should connect to the friction we are addressing, with the limits of the pilot evidence kept visible.
  17. Malik: Put the result review in for two weeks. Bring the same measures whether the pilot succeeds or disappoints; we need an actual decision at that meeting.
  18. Iris: The follow-up review will show what changed, what remains uncertain, and what decision follows. The review date does not promise a retention result or automatic expansion.
  19. Malik: The request is clear: one bounded pilot, an explicit trade-off, and a defined review. I will decide on that basis and record any conditions.
  20. Iris: I will preserve the decision record with your choice and conditions. That gives both teams the same account of the decision, including what was approved and what was deferred.
